# 5G-IoT Detection Rules — Suricata + Sigma

This directory turns the findings in [`threat-repository/`](../threat-repository)
into deployable detection content: network-IDS signatures for
[Suricata](https://suricata.io/) and host-log detections in
[Sigma](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma). Every rule is traceable to a
specific `TR-*` finding in
[`threat-repository/data/threats.csv`](../threat-repository/data/threats.csv)
and to the exact byte offsets, packet counts or command lines that justify
it, recorded in
[`evidence/DATASET-EVIDENCE.md`](evidence/DATASET-EVIDENCE.md). Nothing here
is a generic "5G security rule pack" lifted from elsewhere — every signature
was derived by re-measuring this dataset's own captures and bash histories.

## Why this looks different from a typical Suricata rule pack

Suricata has **no native application-layer parser for NGAP, PFCP or GTP-U**
(confirmed against the current Suricata documentation's supported-protocol
list and generic app-layer-keywords reference — there is no `gtp.*`/`pfcp.*`/
`ngap.*` keyword family the way there is for `http.*`, `tls.*`, `dns.*`,
etc.; keywords such as `gtp_type` exist only in **Snort**'s GTP preprocessor
and do not carry over). Rather than write rules against keywords that cannot
be confirmed to compile on a real Suricata install, every 5G-protocol rule
below is built from two portable primitives instead:

1. **Fixed-offset byte/`content` matching against the 3GPP-specified wire
   format.** GTP-U (TS 29.281) and PFCP (TS 29.244) both place their message
   type at a *fixed* byte offset (offset 1) regardless of TEID/SEID or
   optional-header flags — this is exactly as portable as any `content`
   match Suricata supports natively, and does not depend on any 5G-specific
   parser existing at all.
2. **`threshold`/rate primitives** (`threshold: type both|limit, track
   by_src, count N, seconds T;`) for anything that is fundamentally a
   *volume or shape* anomaly rather than a single-packet signature — this
   covers most of the actual attacks in this dataset (floods, storms,
   enumeration bursts), and is exactly how a real SOC would tune these
   rules for their own network's baseline traffic rate regardless of vendor.

The one exception is NGAP over SCTP, where the ASN.1/APER encoding makes a
byte-offset signature genuinely more fragile (see
[`suricata/5g-n2-ngap-sctp.rules`](suricata/5g-n2-ngap-sctp.rules), sid
5010003) — that rule is explicitly marked **EXPERIMENTAL** with a
calibration procedure, rather than presented as production-ready, because
this authoring session had no `suricata` binary available to test it
against (see "What could not be verified" below).

## Directory layout

```

├── evidence/
│   └── DATASET-EVIDENCE.md            <- exact measurements each rule is built from
├── suricata/

│   ├── 5g-n3-gtpu.rules               <- TR-N3-001, TR-N3-002
│   ├── 5g-n4-pfcp.rules               <- TR-N4-001, TR-N4-002, TR-N4-003
│   ├── 5g-sbi-subscriber.rules        <- TR-SUB-001, TR-SUB-003, TR-SUB-004
│   ├── 5g-database-mongodb.rules      <- TR-DB-001, TR-DB-002
│   ├── 5g-ran-arp.rules               <- TR-RAN-002, TR-RAN-003 (+ TR-RAN-001 note)
│   └── 5g-ops-tooling-download.rules  <- TR-OPS-002, TR-N4-003

    ├── virt_privileged_container_recon.yml      <- TR-VIRT-002, TR-VIRT-003
    ├── n4_pfcp_exploit_tool_execution.yml       <- TR-N4-003

    ├── ops_offensive_tooling_download.yml         <- TR-OPS-002
    └── sub_credential_in_process_cli.yml          <- TR-SUB-001, TR-RAN-001
```

|---|---|---|---|---|
| TR-RAN-001 | Rogue UE, cloned SIM credentials | *(see TR-SUB-001/N2-001 rules)* | `sub_credential_in_process_cli.yml` | credential-exposure proxy |
| TR-RAN-002 | Unanswered ARP/RLS storms (3 root causes) | `5g-ran-arp.rules` sid 5060001/5060002 | `ran_recon_arp_scan_tool.yml` | rate + host correlation |

| TR-N2-002 | N2 unauthenticated/unencrypted | `5g-n2-ngap-sctp.rules` sid 5010004 | — | visibility-only |

| TR-SUB-004 | SUPI-to-IP binding disclosed | `5g-sbi-subscriber.rules` sid 5040005 | — | content match |
| TR-DB-001 | Subscriber DB unauthenticated | `5g-database-mongodb.rules` sid 5050001 | — | visibility-only |
| TR-DB-002 | Standard tooling discloses full subscriber base | `5g-database-mongodb.rules` sid 5050002/5050003 | `db_bulk_subscriber_query.yml` | content + rate + process |
| TR-VIRT-001 | Container escape to host filesystem | — (host-only finding) | `virt_container_escape_device_mount.yml` | process/command sequence |
| TR-VIRT-002 | Excess container capabilities/privileged mode | — (host-only finding) | `virt_privileged_container_recon.yml` | process/command |
| TR-VIRT-003 | Container admin socket reachable from workload | — (host-only finding) | `virt_privileged_container_recon.yml` | process/command |
| TR-VIRT-004 | All 3GPP reference points share one L2 domain | — (architectural finding, not an event) | — | *config review, not a rule* |
| TR-VIRT-005 | Container address reuse misdelivers traffic | — (architectural finding, not an event) | — | *config review, not a rule* |
| TR-AI-001 | Training-data poisoning by label flipping | — (host-only finding) | `ai_training_data_poisoning.yml` | process/script pattern |
| TR-AI-002 | Adversarial evasion, single-feature scaling | — (model-internal; no wire/host artefact beyond TR-AI-001's pipeline) | `ai_training_data_poisoning.yml` (pipeline context) | *no independent signature* |
| TR-AI-003 | Three-feature model has no adversarial robustness | — (architectural finding, not an event) | — | *design review, not a rule* |
| TR-AI-004 | Unprotected ML training corpus from live telemetry | — (host-only finding) | `ai_training_data_poisoning.yml` | process/script pattern |
| TR-OPS-001 | Full-fidelity host capture position | — (architectural finding, not an event) | — | *config review, not a rule* |
| TR-OPS-002 | Offensive tooling pulled from the internet | `5g-ops-tooling-download.rules` sid 5070001-5070004 | `ops_offensive_tooling_download.yml` | HTTP content + process |

Nine of the 27 catalogued findings are intentionally left without a rule
(marked *architectural/design review* or *no reliable signature* above) —
these describe a standing property of the deployment's topology or model
design (e.g. "all reference points share one broadcast domain",
"SUCI uses the null protection scheme"), not a discrete event a detector can
fire on. Shipping a rule for these would either never fire (a static
property is not a trigger) or would be dishonestly dressed up as
event-detection when it is really a configuration/architecture audit item.
The repository is clearer about what signature-based detection can and
cannot do by leaving them out rather than padding the count.

## Portability: what is generic vs. lab-specific

Every rule was written to be usable against **any** 5G-core deployment that
speaks the same standard protocols, not just this lab's Docker network:

* No rule matches on this lab's specific IP addresses, MAC addresses,
  subnets or container names.
* GTP-U/PFCP byte offsets are taken directly from the 3GPP specifications
  (TS 29.281, TS 29.244), not reverse-engineered lab quirks — they will hold
  for any compliant implementation.
* SBI content matches use literal 3GPP TS 29.503/29.571 JSON field names
  (`encPermanentKey`, `supi`, `pei`, `ueIpv4Address`), which are
  specification-mandated names, not this lab's invention.
* The MongoDB rules key on Open5GS's fixed `subscribers` collection/`open5gs`
  database naming convention — this is explicitly scoped as "portable across
  Open5GS-based cores", not universal across every possible subscriber-store
  backend, and the README says so rather than overclaiming.
* Port numbers used (2152, 8805, 38412, 27017) are the IANA/3GPP-assigned
  defaults for GTP-U, PFCP, NGAP and (by convention, not by spec) MongoDB —
  standard across vendors, not a lab choice.
* Rate thresholds (`count`/`seconds`) are starting points calibrated against
  this dataset's own baseline-vs-attack traffic ratios (see
  `evidence/DATASET-EVIDENCE.md`) and are explicitly called out in comments
  as needing re-tuning to the target deployment's actual signalling rate —
  no operator should deploy volumetric thresholds without a short baselining
  period on their own network, and the rule comments say this.

## What could not be verified (read before deploying)

* **No `suricata`, `tshark` or `tcpdump` binary was available in the sandbox
  this rule set was authored in.** All Suricata rules were validated for
  *syntactic* correctness with [`idstools`](https://github.com/jasonish/py-idstools)'s
  rule parser (`pip install idstools`), which confirmed every rule in this
  directory parses as valid Snort/Suricata grammar with unique SIDs — but
  this does **not** confirm runtime semantics (e.g. exactly how Suricata's
  `arp` protocol keyword or SCTP payload buffer behave on your installed
  version). **Before production use, run:**
  ```
  suricata -T -c /etc/suricata/suricata.yaml -S detection-rules/suricata/<file>.rules
  ```
  to have Suricata itself validate and report on each rule file, and replay
  a sample of your own traffic through `suricata -r <pcap> -l <logdir> -S
  <file>.rules --runmode single` to confirm real alerts land where expected.
* **The NGAP byte-offset rule (`5g-n2-ngap-sctp.rules` sid 5010003) is
  explicitly marked EXPERIMENTAL.** The procedure-code offset itself is
  spec-guaranteed (see `evidence/DATASET-EVIDENCE.md` §3), but whether
  Suricata's `content`/`offset` keywords see the SCTP DATA-chunk payload at
  the same offset this analysis measured with `scapy` was not confirmed
  against a live Suricata build. Calibrate it yourself:
  1. Capture a short sample of your own NGAP traffic.
  2. `tshark -r sample.pcap -Y sctp -O sctp,ngap` and note the byte offset
     of `procedureCode` within the frame Wireshark shows for the SCTP
     payload.
  3. Adjust the `offset:16` in sid 5010003 to match, or drop the rule
     entirely and rely on sid 5010001/5010002 (rate-based, fully portable,
     no offset assumption).
* **The 8 Sigma rules were validated with [`pySigma`](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/pySigma)**
  (`SigmaCollection.from_yaml`, confirming the detection logic parses and is
  internally consistent) **and successfully compiled end-to-end to Splunk
  SPL** with `sigma convert -t splunk --without-pipeline` as a concrete
  demonstration that the detection logic is executable, not just
  YAML-valid. They were not run against a live EDR/SIEM's actual field
  schema — field names (`Image`, `CommandLine`) follow the Sigma
  "process_creation" standard taxonomy (Sysmon EventID 1 / Linux auditd
  `execve` equivalent field mapping), but you will still need your
  platform's own Sigma pipeline (e.g. `pysigma-backend-splunk`,
  `pysigma-backend-elasticsearch`, or your EDR vendor's Sigma importer) to
  map them to your actual log schema.
* **PFCP/GTP-U message-type byte values were cross-checked against the
  captures in this repository only** (`module0`–`module6`); they were not
  tested against a second, independent 5G-core implementation's capture.
  The 3GPP specifications guarantee the wire format, but implementation
  bugs in a specific vendor's stack are always possible — treat the byte
  values as spec-derived and dataset-confirmed, not vendor-agnostically
  battle-tested.

## Quick start

```bash
# Validate Suricata rule syntax (no live Suricata install needed):
pip install idstools
python3 -c "
import idstools.rule as rule, glob
for f in glob.glob('detection-rules/suricata/*.rules'):
    for line in open(f):
        line = line.strip()
        if line and not line.startswith('#'):
            assert rule.parse(line) is not None, f'FAILED: {line[:80]}'
print('all rules parse OK')
"

# Load into a real Suricata instance:
suricata -T -c /etc/suricata/suricata.yaml -S detection-rules/suricata/5g-n3-gtpu.rules

# Validate + convert Sigma rules (needs sigma-cli + a backend plugin):
pip install sigma-cli pysigma-backend-splunk
sigma convert -t splunk --without-pipeline detection-rules/sigma/*.yml
```

## MITRE mapping

Each rule's `metadata`/`tags` carries a MITRE ATT&CK technique ID, and where
applicable a MITRE ATLAS ID (for the AI/ML findings) or MITRE FiGHT ID (for
5G-specific techniques), consistent with the mapping methodology already
established in
[`threat-repository/MITRE-MAPPING.md`](../threat-repository/MITRE-MAPPING.md)
and [`threat-repository/data/mitre-mappings.csv`](../threat-repository/data/mitre-mappings.csv).
No new mapping decisions were made here beyond what that document already
established — the rules simply carry the same technique IDs the underlying
`TR-*` finding was already tagged with.

# Dataset evidence supporting the Suricata / Sigma rules

This file records the exact, re-derivable measurements that each rule in
`detection-rules/suricata/` and `detection-rules/sigma/` is built from. Every
number here was produced this session by streaming the raw capture files
under `exercises_logs_anonymised/captures/*.pcap` and
`exercises_logs_anonymised/histories/bash_history_v{1,2}` through a
purpose-built analyser (`scapy`-based, no `tshark`/`suricata` binary was
available in the authoring environment — see "How to reproduce" below).
Nothing in this file is inferred from the lab guides or from the prior
threat-repository text alone; it is re-measured directly from the packets
and shell history.

## 1. GTP-U (N3, port 2152) — TR-N3-001 / TR-N3-002

GTP-U header format (3GPP TS 29.281 §5.1): byte 0 = flags, **byte 1 =
message type**, bytes 2–3 = length, bytes 4–7 = TEID. Message type is
therefore always at a fixed offset from the start of the UDP payload,
independent of TEID value or optional-header flags (the optional-header
extension only appends bytes *after* offset 7, never shifts byte 1).

Measured message-type histogram, `module1.pcap` (16.9 MB, 131,027 frames):

| byte@offset1 | meaning (TS 29.281 Table 5.1-1) | count |
|---|---|---|
| `0xFF` (255) | G-PDU (user data) | 352 |
| `0x1A` (26) | Error Indication | 352 |

The ratio is exactly **1:1** — every G-PDU carried on this tunnel is
answered with a GTP-U Error Indication, meaning the receiving endpoint has
no matching PDU session context for the TEID being used. This is the
network-visible signature behind TR-N3-002. (`module0.pcap` shows no
GTP-U traffic on 2152 at all — TR-N3-002 is specific to the module1
capture window.)

## 2. PFCP (N4, port 8805) — TR-N4-001 / TR-N4-002 / TR-N4-003

PFCP header format (3GPP TS 29.244 §7.2.2): byte 0 = flags/version, **byte 1
= message type**, bytes 2–3 = length. Measured message-type histograms
(byte@offset1, UDP/8805 payload):

| Module | Heartbeat Req/Resp (1/2) | Session Establishment Req/Resp (50/51) | Session Modification Req/Resp (52/53) | Session Deletion Req/Resp (54/55) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| module0 | 900 / 900 | 2 / 2 | 2 / 2 | 4 / 4 |
| module1 | 1032 / 1032 | 18 / 18 | 18 / 18 | 22 / 22 |
| module2 | 2728 / 2728 | 0 / 0 | 0 / 0 | 0 / 0 |
| module4 | 240 / 240 | 0 / 0 | 0 / 0 | 0 / 0 |
| module5 | 156 / 156 | 0 / 0 | 0 / 0 | 0 / 0 |
| module6 | 2388 / 2388 | 0 / 0 | 0 / 0 | 0 / 0 |

Heartbeat volume tracks capture duration/UE churn, not attack activity, and
is not alerting material by itself. What matters for TR-N4-001/002/003 is
that PFCP has **no transport-layer authentication of any kind** (3GPP TS
29.244 defines no mandatory integrity/auth mechanism on N4; it is expected
to run over a "trusted" network) — so any host that can reach UDP/8805 can
originate Session Establishment/Modification/Deletion or Association
messages, and the wire cannot distinguish the real SMF from
`bash_history_v2`'s `pfcpExploit.py --nbg-ip <ip> --fuupip <ip>` (lines
116, 126, 195, 203 — see §5). Detection therefore has to be rate/shape
based (an unexpected volume or new source for session-management
messages), not signature-based, which is what the Suricata rules below
implement.

## 3. NGAP (N2, port 38412 / SCTP) — TR-N2-001 / TR-RAN-001

NGAP-PDU is `CHOICE { initiatingMessage, successfulOutcome,
unsuccessfulOutcome }` ASN.1, APER-encoded (3GPP TS 38.413). The
`procedureCode` field is declared `INTEGER (0..255)` in
`NGAP-CommonDataTypes.asn`, so under APER it always occupies exactly one
whole octet regardless of value — it is **not** length-variable the way
most other NGAP fields are. Empirically, in every NGAP PDU observed in this
dataset's SCTP DATA chunks:

* byte 0 = PDU choice tag (`0x00` initiatingMessage, `0x20`
  successfulOutcome, seen; `unsuccessfulOutcome` not observed)
* byte 1 = `procedureCode`

Extracted directly from `module1.pcap`'s SCTP DATA-chunk user data (350
NGAP PDUs on association `38412<->56645`), byte1 histogram cross-referenced
against 3GPP TS 38.413 `NGAP-Constants.asn` (fetched from the Wireshark
ASN.1 module source, which mirrors the 3GPP-published constants table):

| byte1 (decimal) | 3GPP procedure name | count | direction pair observed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | id-DownlinkNASTransport | 108 | initiating only |
| 46 (0x2E) | id-UplinkNASTransport | 90 | initiating only |
| 14 (0x0E) | id-InitialContextSetup | 30 + 30 | initiating + successfulOutcome |
| 15 (0x0F) | id-InitialUEMessage | 20 | initiating only |
| 41 (0x29) | id-UEContextRelease | 18 + 18 | initiating + successfulOutcome |
| 29 (0x1D) | id-PDUSessionResourceSetup | 18 + 18 | initiating + successfulOutcome |

Every value matches its expected 3GPP-defined procedure exactly (e.g.
`id-InitialUEMessage` is gNB→AMF only and never has a successfulOutcome —
consistent with "initiating only" above), which independently confirms the
byte-offset model is correct for this capture, not a coincidence.

**Portability caveat (important):** this offset is measured from the start
of the SCTP DATA chunk's user-data field, *not* from the start of the raw
SCTP packet payload. A Suricata rule that content-matches against the
packet payload Suricata exposes for the `sctp` protocol must account for
the preceding 16-byte SCTP DATA-chunk header (and any other chunks bundled
in the same SCTP packet, e.g. COOKIE-ECHO/SACK), which shifts the effective
offset and is not something this session could verify against a live
Suricata build (no `suricata` binary was available in the sandbox — see
"Errors & dead ends" in the top-level README). The rules that rely on this
byte pattern are therefore shipped marked **EXPERIMENTAL** and the README
explains exactly how to calibrate/validate the offset with `tshark`/
`suricata -r` before trusting them in production. The rate/threshold-based
N2 rules do not depend on this and are fully portable.

## 4. Cleartext subscriber key material on the SBI (N-something) — TR-SUB-001 / TR-SUB-003

TCP/7777 (Open5GS's default plaintext internal SBI port when TLS is not
configured between network functions — a common default in lab and CI
deployments of Open5GS, free5GC and OAI-CN5G, not unique to this lab) carries
literal JSON bodies matching the 3GPP TS 29.503 `AuthenticationInfoRequest`
shape, in cleartext, in **both** `module0.pcap` and `module1.pcap`:

```
{"authenticationMethod":"5G_AKA","encPermanentKey":"9dad38dfd5c81009342d3a992ef7af48",
 "sequenceNumber":{"sqn":"000000000061"},"authenticationManagementField":"8000",
 "encOpcKey":"c9705d1d58b33...
```

The same 32-hex-character `encPermanentKey`/`encOpcKey` pair recurs
identically across both captures (same test subscriber), and despite the
"enc" prefix in the Open5GS/3GPP field name, no encryption scheme is active
in this deployment (`ECIES`/protection-scheme is null), so the values on
the wire are the actual long-term key `K` and `OPc` in plain hex — directly
corroborated by `bash_history_v1:43`'s `docker run … -e KEY=… -e OP=… -e
OP_TYPE=OPC …` invocation, which passes the identical class of secret on a
container command line (§5). This did **not** reappear in `module2.pcap` or
`module6.pcap` (the two flood/spoof-attack captures) — consistent with
those attacks targeting the registration/NGAP layer before a full
`5G-AKA` challenge-response completes, or reusing already-cached vectors.

## 5. Host-level evidence from `bash_history_v1` / `bash_history_v2`

Grepped directly from the two anonymised bash-history files (line numbers
are from the files as committed in this repository):

* **TR-N4-003 — PFCP exploitation tool staged and run:**
  `bash_history_v2:116` `cat pfcpExploit.py | grep -i seid`
  `bash_history_v2:126` `python3 pfcpExploit.py --nbg-ip <nbg-ip> --fuupip <fuupip>`
  `bash_history_v2:201` `find / -name pfcpExploit.py 2>/dev/null`
  `bash_history_v2:203` `python3 pfcpExploit.py --nbg-ip <ip> --fuupip <ip>`
  `bash_history_v1:333,337,340,349` repeated `find` / `grep -Rni "pfcpExploit"` across
  every running container — an incident responder hunting for the tool, which is
  itself good corroborating evidence the tool existed and was run somewhere in the lab.

* **TR-RAN-002 / TR-RAN-003 / recon (module4) — `arp-scan` reconnaissance:**
  `bash_history_v2:132` `which arp-scan`
  `bash_history_v2:133,136` `arp-scan --localnet --interface eth0`
  `bash_history_v2:135` `apt install arp-scan`
  This matches `module4.pcap`'s measured 9,840 ARP requests vs. 118 replies
  (83:1) — a full-subnet ARP sweep is exactly what `arp-scan --localnet`
  generates, and its unanswered-request pattern is indistinguishable *on
  the wire* from the IMSI-flood side effect in `module2`/`module6` (101,159:32
  and 96,285:1174 respectively) — both are "storm" traffic, different root
  cause, same L2 signature, which is why the Suricata rule for this
  pattern is a general ARP-asymmetry detector, not an "IMSI flood" detector.

* **TR-VIRT-001 — container escape to host filesystem:**
  `bash_history_v2:221` `mount -t devtmpfs devtmpfs /dev`
  `bash_history_v2:222` `mount /dev/mapper/ubuntu--vg-ubuntu--lv /mnt`
  `bash_history_v2:223` `chroot /mnt /bin/bash`
  `bash_history_v2:224` `cat /flag.txt`
  A textbook host-disk-device container escape: the container was given (or
  discovered) visibility of a host block device node, mounted the host's
  logical volume, and `chroot`'d into it to reach files (`/flag.txt`)
  outside the container's filesystem namespace entirely.

* **TR-VIRT-002 / TR-VIRT-003 — capability / socket reconnaissance:**
  `bash_history_v2:249` `capsh --print`
  (nearby) `cat /proc/self/status | grep Cap`
  `bash_history_v1:186` `docker ps -q | while read c; do … docker inspect $c | grep docker.sock; done`
  `bash_history_v2:...` `docker inspect <ctr> --format='Privileged={{.HostConfig.Privileged}}'`
  This is the reconnaissance phase that *precedes* TR-VIRT-001: enumerating
  Linux capabilities, privileged flags and Docker-socket bind-mounts across
  every running container to find an escape path.

* **TR-SUB-001 — long-term key exposed on a process command line:**
  `bash_history_v1:43` a `docker run` invocation carrying
  `-e KEY=716443940B35EC8A7EDA57F98A9A70E6 -e OP_TYPE=OPC
  -e OP=A14B42703DEBABBE206D7597D93D65FA -e MSISDN=0467136224 …` — the
  subscriber's permanent key and OPc are passed as environment variables on
  a shell command line, which lands in `.bash_history`, in `ps aux`/`/proc/<pid>/environ`
  output, and in Docker's own container-inspect metadata — three
  independent plaintext-at-rest exposures of the same secret confirmed on
  the host side, corroborating the wire-side finding in §4.

* **TR-N2-001 / custom replay tooling (module3):**
  `bash_history_v2` (around the `5greplay` block): `wget
  https://github.com/Montimage/5GReplay/releases/download/v0.0.1/5greplay-0.0.1_Linux_x86_64.tar.gz`,
  `tar -xzf …`, `chmod +x 5greplay`, `./5greplay --version`, then `wget
  https://github.com/Montimage/5GReplay/raw/main/docs/docs/tutorial/replay-5geo5og/5g-sa.pcap`
  — a third-party 5G-replay tool and a sample capture pulled from the public
  internet directly onto the core host and made executable, immediately
  before the "custom replay" attack window that produces the 2000x NGAP
  duplication finding in TR-N2-001.

* **TR-AI-001 / TR-AI-002 / TR-AI-004 — IDS training-data poisoning:**
  `bash_history_v2:296-302` `nano train_ids.py`, `python3 train_ids.py`,
  `nano train_poisoned_ids.py`, `python3 train_poisoned_ids.py` (run twice,
  i.e. iterated on), plus (later in the same session)
  `from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestClassifier`,
  `flood_train_idx = y_train[y_train == 'ICMP_FLOOD'].index`,
  `perturbed_train = X_train.copy(); perturbed_train.loc[flood_train_idx, …]`,
  `print("Poisoned samples:", flip_count)` — a label-flipping data-poisoning
  attack against an IDS training set is scripted, executed and its own
  output (`flip_count`) is inspected interactively.

## 6. MongoDB wire-protocol confirmation (TR-DB-001 / TR-DB-002)

MongoDB Wire Protocol header (16 bytes): `messageLength(4)`,
`requestID(4)`, `responseTo(4)`, `opCode(4)`, all little-endian. Every
Mongo packet sampled from `module0`, `module1`, `module2`, `module4`,
`module5`, `module6` on TCP/27017 has `opCode` (bytes 12–15) equal to
`DD 07 00 00` (2013 decimal = `OP_MSG`, the only opcode modern MongoDB
(3.6+) uses for both commands and their responses). No SCRAM/`saslStart`
authentication command was observed in any sampled payload — only
`isMaster`/`hello`+`isWritablePrimary` handshake documents — consistent
with TR-DB-001's finding that authentication is not enabled on this
instance. Open5GS's MongoDB schema uses a fixed, project-wide collection
name, `subscribers`, in a database also conventionally named `open5gs` —
this is a property of the Open5GS codebase, not of this lab's
configuration, so a rule keyed on the literal string `"subscribers"`
inside an `OP_MSG` command document is portable to any Open5GS-based 5G
core, not just this dataset.

## How to reproduce these measurements

No `tshark`, `tcpdump` or `suricata` binary was available in the sandbox
this analysis was performed in. All of the above was produced with
`scapy`'s streaming `PcapReader` (constant memory, safe for the 30–55 MB
capture files in this dataset) — install with
`pip install scapy` and re-run against any of
`exercises_logs_anonymised/captures/module*.pcap`. The two ad-hoc scripts
used are not part of the shipped detection content (they are exploratory
tooling, not a maintained analyser), but their logic is fully described in
this document so the byte offsets and counts above can be independently
re-derived or re-checked against a newer/different NITRO dataset capture.

title: Subscriber Permanent Key/OPc Passed as Process Command-Line Argument
id: ac6002a7-a1bf-4fec-aa63-84fffb372cbf
status: experimental
description: |
  Detects a process (typically a UE simulator or provisioning script)
  launched with a 5G-AKA long-term key (K) and/or OPc value passed directly
  as a command-line argument or environment variable, observed verbatim in
  this dataset's bash history (`docker run ... -e KEY=<32 hex chars>
  -e OP_TYPE=OPC -e OP=<32 hex chars> -e MSISDN=... ...`). Any such secret
  becomes readable to every local user via `ps aux`/`/proc/<pid>/cmdline`,
  is written to shell history, and (for containers) is stored in the
  container engine's own inspect metadata - three independent plaintext
  exposures of the same value. This directly corroborates the wire-side
  finding that the identical key pair is also sent in cleartext over the
  SBI (TR-SUB-001, detection-rules/suricata/5g-sbi-subscriber.rules). The
  32-hex-character K/OPc shape is a 3GPP-standard format (TS 35.206), not
  lab-specific, so the hex-length heuristic generalises to any UE
  simulator/provisioning tool that accepts K/OPc via env vars or CLI flags
  (UERANSIM, srsRAN, OAI UE, gradiant/openairinterface-ue, etc.).
references:
  - internal://5G-IoT-Threat-Repository/threat-repository/data/threats.csv#TR-SUB-001
  - internal://5G-IoT-Threat-Repository/detection-rules/evidence/DATASET-EVIDENCE.md#5-host-level-evidence
author: 5G-IoT Threat Repository project
date: 2026-09-30
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: linux
detection:
  selection_env_key_opc:
    CommandLine|contains|all:
      - 'KEY='
      - 'OP='
  selection_op_type_opc:
    CommandLine|contains: 'OP_TYPE=OPC'
  selection_hex32_pattern:
    CommandLine|re: '(KEY|OP|OPC)=[0-9A-Fa-f]{32}\b'
  condition: (selection_env_key_opc and selection_op_type_opc) or selection_hex32_pattern
falsepositives:
  - Lab/CI environments that intentionally provision synthetic test
    subscribers this way - still worth flagging as a hardening finding even
    when non-malicious, since the same mechanism is what leaks a real
    subscriber's key if test and production provisioning paths are ever
    reused (exactly what TR-RAN-001 observed).
level: high

detection-rules/suricata/5g-database-mongodb.rules
+38
# =============================================================================
# Subscriber-database (MongoDB, TCP/27017) detection rules
# Threat-repository mapping: TR-DB-001, TR-DB-002
# Evidence: detection-rules/evidence/DATASET-EVIDENCE.md section 6
#
# MongoDB Wire Protocol header is 16 bytes, little-endian:
#   messageLength(4) requestID(4) responseTo(4) opCode(4)
# opCode == 2013 (OP_MSG, hex bytes DD 07 00 00 little-endian) is the only
# opcode used by MongoDB 3.6+ for both commands and query responses, so
# matching it at the fixed offset 12 is stable and version-independent for
# any modern MongoDB deployment - not specific to this lab.
# =============================================================================
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# TR-DB-001: subscriber database accepts unauthenticated queries. Suricata
# cannot prove "no authentication" from the wire alone (SCRAM auth also
# rides inside OP_MSG frames), so this is shipped as a visibility rule: any
# MongoDB wire-protocol traffic reaching an interface that is not the
# database's own management network is worth an analyst's attention in a
# 5G core, since UDM/subscriber storage should never be reachable from
# arbitrary NFs or the RAN side. Pair with your own network's expected
# DB-tier host list (Suricata `ipvar DB_TIER`) to turn this into a hard
# violation rule for your specific deployment; left generic here.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert tcp any any -> any 27017 (msg:"5G-IOT MongoDB (subscriber store) wire-protocol traffic observed (verify source is expected DB-tier client, TR-DB-001)"; content:"|DD 07 00 00|"; offset:12; depth:4; threshold: type limit, track by_src, count 1, seconds 300; classtype:policy-violation; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-DB-001, mitre_attack T1552, confidence portable-visibility; reference:url,mongodb.com/docs/manual/reference/mongodb-wire-protocol; sid:5050001; rev:1;)
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# TR-DB-002: standard subscriber-management tooling discloses the full
# subscriber base. Open5GS's MongoDB schema always names its subscriber
# collection "subscribers" (a project-wide constant, not lab-specific), so a
# `find`/query command document referencing it is a reliable, portable
# indicator of subscriber-record access on any Open5GS-based core. A high
# rate of such commands from one client in a short window indicates bulk
# enumeration/extraction rather than a single subscriber lookup.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert tcp any any -> any 27017 (msg:"5G-IOT MongoDB subscriber collection ('subscribers') accessed (TR-DB-002)"; content:"|DD 07 00 00|"; offset:12; depth:4; content:"subscribers"; distance:0; nocase; classtype:sensitive-data; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-DB-002, mitre_attack T1213, confidence portable-open5gs; sid:5050002; rev:1;)
alert tcp any any -> any 27017 (msg:"5G-IOT MongoDB bulk subscriber-collection query burst - possible mass extraction (TR-DB-002)"; content:"|DD 07 00 00|"; offset:12; depth:4; content:"subscribers"; distance:0; nocase; threshold: type both, track by_src, count 20, seconds 10; classtype:sensitive-data; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-DB-002, mitre_attack T1213, confidence portable-open5gs; sid:5050003; rev:1;)

detection-rules/suricata/5g-n2-ngap-sctp.rules
+70
# =============================================================================
# 5G N2 (NGAP over SCTP, port 38412) detection rules
# Threat-repository mapping: TR-N2-001, TR-N2-002, TR-RAN-001
# Evidence: detection-rules/evidence/DATASET-EVIDENCE.md sections 3 and 5
#
# Portability: rules 5010001-5010002 use only port + SCTP chunk-type/packet
# rate primitives that are present in every Suricata build since SCTP support
# was added (Suricata >= 2.x) and require no NGAP-aware parser. Rule 5010003
# is content-offset based and is marked EXPERIMENTAL - read the comment above
# it and detection-rules/README.md#calibrating-the-experimental-ngap-rule
# before enabling it anywhere outside a lab.
# =============================================================================
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# TR-N2-001: NGAP replay flood against the AMF (2000x duplication observed).
# Generic signature: a sustained high rate of SCTP packets on the NGAP SCTP
# port from a single gNB/UE-simulator association. Tune COUNT/SECONDS to the
# expected steady-state signalling rate of the deployment being protected;
# the values below are set well above any legitimate single-association
# NGAP rate (module1 baseline: 350 NGAP PDUs across an entire ~46-minute
# capture, i.e. roughly 0.13 PDU/s per association).
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert sctp any any -> any 38412 (msg:"5G-IOT N2 NGAP sustained high packet rate toward AMF (possible replay/registration flood, TR-N2-001)"; flow:to_server; threshold: type both, track by_src, count 200, seconds 5; classtype:attempted-dos; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-N2-001, mitre_attack T1499, mitre_fight FGT1499.003, confidence experimental-threshold, evidence "module1.pcap SCTP assoc 38412<->56645; module3 custom-replay case study"; reference:url,3gpp.org/ftp/Specs/archive/38_series/38.413; sid:5010001; rev:1;)
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# TR-N2-001 (companion): SCTP association-setup (INIT) flood toward the AMF's
# NGAP listener - repeated association attempts from the same or many
# sources is the classic precursor/companion of an NGAP replay or
# registration-storm attack, and is fully protocol-generic (works against
# any SCTP-based control interface, not just NGAP).
# NOTE: matches on the 1-byte SCTP chunk-type field, which is always the
# first byte of the chunk area immediately following the 12-byte common
# header, per RFC 9260 (SCTP) section 3.2 - INIT chunk type = 1 (0x01).
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert sctp any any -> any 38412 (msg:"5G-IOT N2 SCTP INIT flood toward NGAP listener (association-setup storm, TR-N2-001)"; content:"|01|"; offset:0; depth:1; threshold: type both, track by_src, count 20, seconds 5; classtype:attempted-dos; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-N2-001, mitre_attack T1499.002, confidence portable; reference:url,datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc9260; sid:5010002; rev:1;)
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# TR-N2-001 / TR-RAN-001 (EXPERIMENTAL): id-InitialUEMessage flood at the
# NGAP-PDU byte level. procedureCode=15 (id-InitialUEMessage) at byte 1 of
# the NGAP-PDU is spec-guaranteed stable (3GPP TS 38.413 NGAP-Constants.asn;
# procedureCode is APER-encoded as a full INTEGER(0..255) octet) and was
# verified against this dataset's own module1.pcap traffic (20 occurrences,
# all initiatingMessage, matching id-InitialUEMessage's gNB->AMF-only
# semantics exactly - see evidence doc section 3).
#
# CAVEAT: the offsets below assume the NGAP-PDU begins immediately after a
# 16-byte SCTP DATA-chunk header with no other chunks bundled in the same
# packet. This chunk-header/offset interaction was NOT verified against a
# live Suricata binary (none was available while authoring this rule set).
# Before deploying: capture a short sample of your own NGAP traffic, confirm
# with `tshark -r sample.pcap -O sctp,ngap` that DATA chunks in your
# environment are single-chunk-per-packet, then validate this rule with
# `suricata -r sample.pcap -l /tmp/out -S this_file --runmode single` and
# check that eve.json alerts land on genuine InitialUEMessage frames before
# trusting it in production. Prefer sid:5010001/5010002 above if you cannot
# validate this offset in your own environment.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert sctp any any -> any 38412 (msg:"5G-IOT N2 NGAP InitialUEMessage burst - possible registration/replay flood (EXPERIMENTAL offset, TR-N2-001)"; content:"|00|"; offset:16; depth:1; content:"|0F|"; distance:0; within:1; threshold: type both, track by_src, count 50, seconds 5; classtype:attempted-dos; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-N2-001, mitre_attack T1499.002, confidence experimental-byte-offset; reference:url,3gpp.org/ftp/Specs/archive/38_series/38.413; sid:5010003; rev:1;)
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# TR-N2-002: N2 carries NGAP/NAS with no IPsec, DTLS-over-SCTP or NGAP-level
# anti-replay. This is an absence-of-control finding, not a wire signature -
# there is no packet pattern that proves "no encryption" other than the
# traffic simply being plaintext NGAP on 38412 in the first place. We flag
# it as a one-shot visibility/inventory alert per new association so an
# analyst can confirm whether DTLS was expected here at all; it is
# deliberately low-volume (type limit, count 1) so it functions as
# "new unprotected N2 association observed" rather than noise.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert sctp any any -> any 38412 (msg:"5G-IOT N2 NGAP association observed without SCTP-DTLS (unauthenticated/unencrypted N2, TR-N2-002)"; content:"|01|"; offset:0; depth:1; threshold: type limit, track by_src, count 1, seconds 3600; classtype:policy-violation; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-N2-002, mitre_attack T1040, confidence portable-visibility-only; sid:5010004; rev:1;)

detection-rules/suricata/5g-n3-gtpu.rules
+55
# =============================================================================
# 5G N3 (GTP-U user plane, UDP/2152) detection rules
# Threat-repository mapping: TR-N3-001, TR-N3-002
# Evidence: detection-rules/evidence/DATASET-EVIDENCE.md section 1
#
# GTP-U header (3GPP TS 29.281 section 5.1): byte 0 = flags, byte 1 =
# message type, bytes 2-3 = length, bytes 4-7 = TEID. Message-type offset is
# fixed regardless of optional-header flags (those only append bytes after
# offset 7). This makes GTP-U, unlike NGAP, safe for direct `content`
# matching on the UDP payload with no chunk/ASN.1 ambiguity - Suricata has
# no native GTP-U parser (see detection-rules/README.md), so these rules use
# raw byte-offset matching instead of any GTP-specific keyword.
# =============================================================================
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# TR-N3-002: user-plane desynchronisation - a GTP-U peer answers G-PDUs with
# Error Indication (message type 0x1A/26) because it holds no session
# context for the TEID in use. A single Error Indication is unremarkable
# (can happen during normal handover/teardown races); a *sustained* stream
# of them from the same peer, at a rate comparable to the G-PDU traffic
# itself, indicates either a stale/hijacked TEID or an active enumeration
# attempt (see TR-N3-001 below). Baseline in this dataset: 352 G-PDU vs 352
# Error Indication in module1 (an exact 1:1 ratio) - tune COUNT to a level
# clearly above incidental teardown noise for the target deployment.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert udp any any -> any 2152 (msg:"5G-IOT N3 GTP-U Error Indication storm (user-plane desync / stale or probed TEID, TR-N3-002)"; content:"|1A|"; offset:1; depth:1; threshold: type both, track by_src, count 20, seconds 10; classtype:bad-unknown; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-N3-002, mitre_attack T1499, confidence portable; reference:url,3gpp.org/ftp/Specs/archive/29_series/29.281; sid:5020001; rev:1;)
alert udp any 2152 -> any any (msg:"5G-IOT N3 GTP-U Error Indication storm from UPF/gNB peer (TR-N3-002)"; content:"|1A|"; offset:1; depth:1; threshold: type both, track by_src, count 20, seconds 10; classtype:bad-unknown; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-N3-002, mitre_attack T1499, confidence portable; sid:5020002; rev:1;)
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# TR-N3-001: GTP-U tunnel endpoint identifiers (TEIDs) enumerated from the
# capture position. Suricata's `content`/`threshold` primitives can count
# *packets* per source but cannot natively track the *cardinality* of a
# 4-byte field (distinct TEID values) the way a flow analytics tool (Zeek,
# NetFlow/IPFIX with TEID export, or a custom eve.json post-processor) can.
# What Suricata *can* flag portably is a high rate of GTP-U Error
# Indications carrying different TEIDs from the same source in a short
# window, which is the observable side-effect of TEID probing (each guessed
# TEID that does not match a live session yields a fresh Error Indication).
# This rule is intentionally the same shape as TR-N3-002's (an Error
# Indication rate rule) because on the wire the two conditions overlap -
# use eve.json's captured TEID field values (Suricata logs the raw packet;
# a downstream script can extract bytes 4-7 of the UDP payload) to
# distinguish "one stale tunnel" (few distinct TEIDs) from "TEID scan"
# (many distinct TEIDs) during triage. See detection-rules/README.md for a
# short reference script.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert udp any any -> any 2152 (msg:"5G-IOT N3 GTP-U echo/error rate anomaly - possible TEID enumeration (TR-N3-001, verify distinct TEID count downstream)"; content:"|1A|"; offset:1; depth:1; threshold: type both, track by_src, count 50, seconds 30; classtype:attempted-recon; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-N3-001, mitre_attack T1046, confidence portable-needs-triage; sid:5020003; rev:1;)
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Companion visibility rule: GTP-U Echo Request/Response flood (message
# types 1/2) - a generic liveness-check abuse pattern applicable to any
# GTP-U-speaking element (gNB, UPF), independent of this lab's topology.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert udp any any -> any 2152 (msg:"5G-IOT N3 GTP-U Echo Request flood (peer liveness-check abuse)"; content:"|01|"; offset:1; depth:1; threshold: type both, track by_src, count 100, seconds 10; classtype:attempted-dos; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-N3-002, mitre_attack T1499, confidence portable; sid:5020004; rev:1;)

detection-rules/suricata/5g-n4-pfcp.rules
+57
# =============================================================================
# 5G N4 (PFCP, UDP/8805) detection rules
# Threat-repository mapping: TR-N4-001, TR-N4-002, TR-N4-003
# Evidence: detection-rules/evidence/DATASET-EVIDENCE.md sections 2 and 5
#
# PFCP header (3GPP TS 29.244 section 7.2.2): byte 0 = flags/version, byte 1
# = message type, bytes 2-3 = length. Suricata has no native PFCP parser, so
# these rules use raw byte-offset matching on the UDP payload, exactly as
# with the N3/GTP-U rules above.
#
# Because PFCP has no authentication mechanism at all (TS 29.244 assumes a
# "trusted" N4 network), a signature that tries to say "this PFCP message is
# unauthorised" cannot exist on the wire - every message looks equally
# legitimate syntactically. What *can* be detected portably is rate/shape
# anomalies consistent with automated exploitation or enumeration tooling
# such as bash_history_v2's `pfcpExploit.py --nbg-ip <ip> --fuupip <ip>`
# (see evidence doc section 5) rather than normal one-session-at-a-time SMF
# signalling.
# =============================================================================
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# TR-N4-002 / TR-N4-003: PFCP Session Establishment Request (type 50) burst
# from a single source. TR-N4-002 notes the SEID space in this deployment is
# effectively only 12 bits (4096 values) - a brute-force/enumeration tool
# can therefore complete a full sweep in seconds, producing a Session
# Establishment (or Modification/Deletion) request rate far above what a
# real SMF generates (one request per UE PDU-session event).
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert udp any any -> any 8805 (msg:"5G-IOT N4 PFCP Session Establishment Request burst - possible SEID enumeration/exploit tooling (TR-N4-002/TR-N4-003)"; content:"|32|"; offset:1; depth:1; threshold: type both, track by_src, count 10, seconds 5; classtype:attempted-recon; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-N4-002, mitre_attack T1110, confidence portable; reference:url,3gpp.org/ftp/Specs/archive/29_series/29.244; sid:5030001; rev:1;)
alert udp any any -> any 8805 (msg:"5G-IOT N4 PFCP Session Modification Request burst (possible session hijack/manipulation, TR-N4-002/TR-N4-003)"; content:"|34|"; offset:1; depth:1; threshold: type both, track by_src, count 15, seconds 5; classtype:attempted-admin; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-N4-002, mitre_attack T1565, confidence portable; sid:5030002; rev:1;)
alert udp any any -> any 8805 (msg:"5G-IOT N4 PFCP Session Deletion Request burst (possible mass session teardown DoS, TR-N4-002/TR-N4-003)"; content:"|36|"; offset:1; depth:1; threshold: type both, track by_src, count 15, seconds 5; classtype:attempted-dos; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-N4-002, mitre_attack T1499, confidence portable; sid:5030003; rev:1;)
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# TR-N4-001: PFCP session management unauthenticated - a new PFCP
# Association Setup Request (type 5) announces a *new* N4 peer (candidate
# SMF or UPF). A legitimate deployment establishes its SMF<->UPF
# association(s) once at startup; a burst of Association Setup Requests, or
# one arriving mid-operation from a source that has not been seen on N4
# before, is consistent with a rogue/impersonating N4 peer (which is the
# behaviour `pfcpExploit.py` targets per its `--nbg-ip`/`--fuupip` flags -
# "new bogus gNB/UPF"-style peer injection - see evidence doc section 5).
# This rule flags the volume signal; pair it with the SMF/UPF allow-list
# your deployment already has (Suricata `ipvar N4_PEERS`s) if you want to
# also flag a *source* the wire never expected - deliberately left generic
# here since expected N4 peers are environment-specific and cannot be
# hardcoded into a rule meant to be reused elsewhere.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert udp any any -> any 8805 (msg:"5G-IOT N4 PFCP Association Setup Request from new/unexpected peer (rogue SMF/UPF impersonation, TR-N4-001/TR-N4-003)"; content:"|05|"; offset:1; depth:1; threshold: type limit, track by_src, count 1, seconds 300; classtype:attempted-admin; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-N4-001, mitre_attack T1565.002, confidence portable-visibility; sid:5030004; rev:1;)
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# TR-N4-001 (companion): PFCP heartbeat flood - low-effort denial-of-service
# against the N4 control channel using only Heartbeat Request (type 1),
# which requires no session context and is accepted from anyone by design.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert udp any any -> any 8805 (msg:"5G-IOT N4 PFCP Heartbeat Request flood (unauthenticated N4, TR-N4-001/TR-N4-002)"; content:"|01|"; offset:1; depth:1; threshold: type both, track by_src, count 300, seconds 10; classtype:attempted-dos; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-N4-002, mitre_attack T1499, confidence portable; sid:5030005; rev:1;)

detection-rules/suricata/5g-ops-tooling-download.rules
+30
# =============================================================================
# Offensive-tooling download detection (HTTP)
# Threat-repository mapping: TR-OPS-002
# Evidence: detection-rules/evidence/DATASET-EVIDENCE.md section 5
#
# bash_history_v2 shows a 5G-replay tool and a sample attack pcap pulled
# directly from a public GitHub release onto the core host with `wget`,
# `chmod +x` and then executed - immediately before the NGAP-replay attack
# window (TR-N2-001/module3). These rules use Suricata's native HTTP
# app-layer buffers (`http.uri`, `http.user_agent`) rather than raw TCP
# content, so they are transport-detail independent and will match this
# traffic pattern on any deployment, on any port HTTP is spoken on - not
# tied to this lab's addressing.
# =============================================================================
alert http any any -> any any (msg:"5G-IOT OPS offensive 5G-replay tooling downloaded onto host (5GReplay release artifact, TR-OPS-002)"; flow:established,to_server; http.uri; content:"5greplay"; nocase; classtype:policy-violation; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-OPS-002, mitre_attack T1105, mitre_fight FGT1105, confidence portable; reference:url,github.com/Montimage/5GReplay; sid:5070001; rev:1;)
alert http any any -> any any (msg:"5G-IOT OPS PFCP exploitation tool reference fetched over HTTP (TR-N4-003/TR-OPS-002)"; flow:established,to_server; http.uri; content:"pfcpExploit"; nocase; classtype:policy-violation; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-OPS-002, mitre_attack T1105, confidence portable; sid:5070002; rev:1;)
alert http any any -> any any (msg:"5G-IOT OPS network reconnaissance tool package fetched over HTTP (arp-scan, TR-OPS-002)"; flow:established,to_server; http.uri; content:"arp-scan"; nocase; classtype:policy-violation; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-OPS-002, mitre_attack T1105, confidence portable; sid:5070003; rev:1;)
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Generic companion: any executable artifact (`.tar.gz`/no-extension ELF)
# fetched from a raw GitHub releases/raw-content host directly to a host
# already running 5G core control-plane processes is worth flagging
# independent of the specific tool name, since legitimate 5G core software
# supply chains use container registries and CI pipelines, not interactive
# wget on the running core host.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert http any any -> any any (msg:"5G-IOT OPS executable/archive fetched from github.com release/raw content directly onto core host (TR-OPS-002)"; flow:established,to_server; http.host; content:"github.com"; nocase; http.uri; content:"releases/download"; nocase; classtype:policy-violation; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-OPS-002, mitre_attack T1105, confidence portable; sid:5070004; rev:1;)

detection-rules/suricata/5g-ran-arp.rules
+57
# =============================================================================
# RAN-side L2/ARP detection rules
# Threat-repository mapping: TR-RAN-002, TR-RAN-003
# Evidence: detection-rules/evidence/DATASET-EVIDENCE.md sections 1 and 5
#
# Suricata added a native `arp` protocol keyword and ARP decoder/logger in
# Suricata 8.0 (mid-2025); the `arp` protocol token itself has existed in
# the generic rule-protocol list for longer, but full field-level keywords
# (arp.opcode etc.) are only guaranteed present on 8.0+. To stay portable
# across older, still widely deployed Suricata 6.x/7.x installations, rules
# 5060001/5060002 below match the fixed RFC 826 Ethernet/IPv4 ARP wire
# format directly with `content`/`offset` (hwtype=0x0001, protype=0x0800,
# hwlen=6, protolen=4, opcode=1 request / 2 reply - bytes 0-7 of the ARP
# payload), which works on any Suricata build that accepts `arp` as a
# protocol token. If you are on Suricata >= 8.0, the commented `arp.opcode`
# variants immediately below each rule are the more idiomatic equivalent -
# uncomment and disable the byte-offset version if you have verified your
# build supports them (`suricata --list-keywords | grep arp`).
#
# Two lab-verified, independent root causes both produce this exact
# request/reply-asymmetry signature: a 1,000-simulated-UE registration flood
# (module2: 102,524 req / 1,378 reply) and full-subnet reconnaissance with
# `arp-scan --localnet` (module4: 9,840 req / 118 reply; see
# bash_history_v2:132-136). The rule intentionally does not try to
# distinguish them - both are "storm" conditions worth alerting on - and
# leaves cause attribution to the analyst using nearby NGAP/registration
# volume (a flood) versus a single host's scanning cadence (recon) as the
# distinguishing context, exactly as documented in the evidence file.
# =============================================================================
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# ARP request storm from a single source (flood or subnet sweep precursor).
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert arp any any -> any any (msg:"5G-IOT RAN ARP request storm - possible UE/registration flood or subnet reconnaissance (TR-RAN-002/TR-RAN-003)"; content:"|00 01 08 00 06 04 00 01|"; offset:0; depth:8; threshold: type both, track by_src, count 200, seconds 5; classtype:attempted-dos; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-RAN-002, mitre_attack T1046, mitre_fight FGT1046, confidence portable; reference:url,datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc826; sid:5060001; rev:1;)
# Suricata >= 8.0 idiomatic equivalent (uncomment if supported):
# alert arp any any -> any any (msg:"5G-IOT RAN ARP request storm (TR-RAN-002/TR-RAN-003)"; arp.opcode:1; threshold: type both, track by_src, count 200, seconds 5; classtype:attempted-dos; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-RAN-002; sid:5060001; rev:1;)
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Companion: the request:reply asymmetry itself is the real anomaly (a
# baseline network answers most of its ARP requests). Suricata cannot
# compute a live ratio between two rule counters natively; the recommended
# deployment is to alert on both this rule and 5060001, and compute
# request:reply ratio in the SIEM/eve.json pipeline over the same window
# (this dataset's own ratios: 1,000-UE flood ~74:1, arp-scan recon ~83:1,
# both dramatically above a healthy LAN's near-1:1 baseline).
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert arp any any -> any any (msg:"5G-IOT RAN ARP reply activity (baseline counter - correlate volume against sid:5060001 for request:reply ratio, TR-RAN-002)"; content:"|00 01 08 00 06 04 00 02|"; offset:0; depth:8; threshold: type limit, track by_src, count 1, seconds 5; classtype:not-suspicious; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-RAN-002, confidence portable; sid:5060002; rev:1;)
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# TR-RAN-001: rogue UE with cloned credentials attaching to a live gNB. No
# reliable L2/L3 signature exists for this (the UE simulator behaves like
# any legitimate UE on the wire); detection instead relies on the
# NGAP/SBI-layer rules in 5g-n2-ngap-sctp.rules and 5g-sbi-subscriber.rules
# plus the host-level Sigma rule for credential exposure on a container
# command line (sigma/sub_credential_in_process_cli.yml). Documented here so
# the mapping from TR-ID to "where the actual detection lives" is not lost.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

detection-rules/suricata/5g-sbi-subscriber.rules
+56
# =============================================================================
# 5G Service-Based Interface (SBI) subscriber-data exposure rules
# Threat-repository mapping: TR-SUB-001, TR-SUB-003, TR-SUB-004
# Evidence: detection-rules/evidence/DATASET-EVIDENCE.md section 4
#
# All content strings below are literal 3GPP TS 29.503/29.571 JSON field
# names used across the Nudm/Nausf/Namf/Nsmf SBI APIs (AuthenticationInfo,
# SubscriberIdentity, etc.) - they are standard field names defined by the
# specification and implemented under those exact names by every major
# open-source 5G core (Open5GS, free5GC, OAI-CN5G), so these rules are not
# tied to this lab's specific IP addresses or port numbers; they will match
# on cleartext SBI traffic on *any* port. If your deployment terminates the
# SBI over TLS end-to-end (as production networks should, per TS 33.501),
# these rules correctly will not fire - a plaintext SBI transaction should
# not exist on a hardened deployment, so their absence of alerts is itself
# a useful negative-control signal during a security assessment.
#
# No port restriction is applied deliberately: this lab exposes SBI on
# TCP/7777 (Open5GS's default plaintext internal SBI port), but other
# deployments may use 80/8080/others when TLS is not (mis)configured.
# =============================================================================
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# TR-SUB-001: long-term SIM keys (K / OPc) recoverable in cleartext from an
# Nudm-UEAU AuthenticationInfoRequest/Result-shaped JSON body.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert tcp any any -> any any (msg:"5G-IOT SBI cleartext subscriber permanent key (encPermanentKey) observed on service-based interface (TR-SUB-001)"; flow:established; content:"encPermanentKey"; nocase; classtype:sensitive-data; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-SUB-001, mitre_attack T1552, mitre_fight FGT1552, confidence portable; reference:url,3gpp.org/ftp/Specs/archive/29_series/29.503; sid:5040001; rev:1;)
alert tcp any any -> any any (msg:"5G-IOT SBI cleartext subscriber OPc key (encOpcKey) observed on service-based interface (TR-SUB-001)"; flow:established; content:"encOpcKey"; nocase; classtype:sensitive-data; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-SUB-001, mitre_attack T1552, confidence portable; sid:5040002; rev:1;)
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# TR-SUB-003: SUPI and IMEISV traverse the SBI in cleartext. `"supi":"imsi-`
# and `"pei":"imeisv-` are the literal 3GPP TS 29.571 CommonData field/value
# prefixes used in every SBI body that carries a subscriber or PEI
# identifier (Namf_Communication, Namf_EventExposure, Nudm_SDM, etc).
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert tcp any any -> any any (msg:"5G-IOT SBI cleartext SUPI (imsi-*) observed on service-based interface (TR-SUB-003)"; flow:established; content:"|22|supi|22|"; nocase; content:"imsi-"; distance:0; within:40; classtype:sensitive-data; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-SUB-003, mitre_attack T1071.001, confidence portable; sid:5040003; rev:1;)
alert tcp any any -> any any (msg:"5G-IOT SBI cleartext PEI/IMEISV (imeisv-*) observed on service-based interface (TR-SUB-003)"; flow:established; content:"|22|pei|22|"; nocase; content:"imeisv-"; distance:0; within:40; classtype:sensitive-data; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-SUB-003, mitre_attack T1071.001, confidence portable; sid:5040004; rev:1;)
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# TR-SUB-004: SUPI-to-IP binding disclosed on the SBI, enabling subscriber
# traffic attribution. Matches an SBI body that carries both a SUPI and a
# UE IP address field together (Nsmf_PDUSession / N4 session-context
# exposure over SBI, per TS 29.502/29.508 field names).
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert tcp any any -> any any (msg:"5G-IOT SBI SUPI-to-IP binding disclosed (subscriber traffic attribution, TR-SUB-004)"; flow:established; content:"|22|supi|22|"; nocase; content:"ueIpv4Address"; distance:0; within:500; classtype:sensitive-data; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-SUB-004, mitre_attack T1040, confidence portable; sid:5040005; rev:1;)
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Companion rate rule: a burst of distinct AuthenticationInfoRequest bodies
# from one source in a short window is consistent with automated harvesting
# of authentication vectors rather than a single UE's normal AKA run
# (module1/module0 baseline: a handful of AKA runs across the whole
# capture window, not a burst).
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
alert tcp any any -> any any (msg:"5G-IOT SBI repeated cleartext AKA vector retrieval - possible bulk key harvesting (TR-SUB-001)"; flow:established; content:"authenticationMethod"; nocase; content:"encPermanentKey"; distance:0; within:200; threshold: type both, track by_src, count 10, seconds 30; classtype:sensitive-data; metadata: threat_repo_id TR-SUB-001, mitre_attack T1552, confidence portable; sid:5040006; rev:1;)

# Ground-Truth Cross-Check — NITRO-5G-IOT-BOOKS

**Status:** advisory, first pass, 2026-09-30. Source:
`github.com/dr-skaragiannis/NITRO-5G-IOT-BOOKS` (public, 2 commits, pushed today).

---

## 1. This is the authored attack script

`NOVELTY-ASSESSMENT.md` §9 said the strongest available result depended on finding the exercise
designer's own specification, and that it was not in the dataset. It exists, and it is better
than hoped.

Each module directory carries a **`.json` lab walkthrough** — a structured sequence of steps with
`command`, `expected_output`, `explanation` and, for CTF modules, `associated_flags`. That is a
per-step statement of intent with an expected observable for each step. It is a stronger oracle
than a scenario document, because it declares not only what was run but what the designer
expected to see.

Directory numbering `Module 00`–`Module 09` maps one-to-one onto `module0.pcap`–`module9.pcap`.

### 1a. Authoritative module map

| Module | Designed activity (from the course README and lab guides) |
|---|---|
| 00 | Introduction to Open5GS; gNB SearchList and AMF fundamentals |
| 01 | Ping and basic communication in a 5G network |
| 02 | **IMSI flood / DoS against the AMF** |
| 03 | **Custom replay attack** |
| 04 | **Reconnaissance** |
| 05 | Changing / adding **network slices** |
| 06 | **Cascading effects via slice propagation; session hijacking via IMSI spoofing** |
| 07 | **Docker and host escape** |
| 08 | NetFlow extraction and ML-IDS adversarial AI |
| 09 | Adversarial AI and model robustness |

Stack declared: Open5GS + UERANSIM + Docker Compose, with tshark, nmap, arp-scan, iperf3, scapy,
and Jupyter/pandas/scikit-learn for 08–09. This confirms the environment model in
`threat-repository/ENVIRONMENT.md` independently.

**Module 04's guide (`Module 04 Reconnaissance on 5G.json`) has now been read in full.** Its nine
steps are exactly `docker ps` → `docker network inspect` → `nmap -sn` (host discovery) →
`nmap -p- -sV` (port/service scan) → `nmap --script vulners` (CVE lookup against detected service
versions) → `tshark -i any -f 'port 38412'` (live SCTP capture) → offline `tshark -Y ngap`
analysis → flag extraction. This is exactly what `threat-repository/rerun-package/attacks/
module04_recon.sh` already reimplements; no changes to that script were needed. Its worked
example, `CVE-2025-15417`, was independently checked against NVD and **is real**: a local DoS in
`sgwc_s11_handle_create_session_request` in Open5GS's GTPv2-C F-TEID handler (affects up to
2.7.6, CVSS 3.1 5.5 Medium per NVD). It is not itself relevant to this dataset's 5G core (which
runs the AMF/SMF/UPF split-core NGAP/PFCP path, not the S11/GTPv2-C SGW-C interface this CVE
affects), but it confirms the guide's CVE-lookup step names a genuine, currently-tracked Open5GS
vulnerability class rather than a fabricated placeholder.

---

## 2. First-pass falsification — four findings look wrong

This is a preliminary read from the module map and the Module 02 lab guide only. It is not the
full pass, but it is already enough to say the reconstruction contains errors.

### 2a. `TR-RAN-002` — wrong cause *(module2)*

**We said:** stale RAN peer causing an RLS and ARP storm — 101,159 ARP requests against 32
replies, `172.18.0.16 → 172.18.0.15`.

**Ground truth:** *IMSI Flood Attack on 5G Core Network.* The lab guide creates `script.sh`
inside `open5gs-and-ueransim-ues1-1` running **5 parallel `nr-ue` instances × 200 UEs = 1,000
simulated UEs**, run two or three times, to exhaust AMF memory and CPU. Declared success flag:
`UE switches to state [deregistered]` / `[error] out of coverage`.

**Verdict:** the ARP and UDP/4997 volume is real, but it is the *side effect* of 1,000 UERANSIM
UEs, not a stale peer. The attack was a NAS registration flood aimed at the AMF. We described
a symptom and inferred the wrong cause — and appear to have under-reported the AMF DoS itself.

### 2b. `TR-DB-002` — possible false positive on a Critical finding *(module5)*

**We said:** bulk subscriber extraction via an ephemeral container — `mongosh 2.4.2` against
`open5gs.subscribers`, unauthenticated.

**Ground truth:** module 05 is *Introduction to Network Slices* — changing and adding slices.
In Open5GS, adding a slice **is** an edit to subscriber documents in MongoDB.

**Verdict:** the observation stands (`TR-DB-001`, unauthenticated Mongo, is unaffected), but
characterising the `mongosh` session as *extraction* may be wrong. It was plausibly the
designed slice-configuration step. A Critical-severity entry may need to be downgraded or
recast as "administrative access path that is indistinguishable on the wire from exfiltration"
— which is a more interesting finding anyway.

**Resolved.** The full module 05 guide (`Module 05 - Introduction to Network Slices.json`, both
chunks) has now been read in full. Its eight designed steps are: stop containers, edit
`smf.yaml` to add a second S-NSSAI with QoS (5QI/ARP/MBR/GBR), edit `amf.yaml` to advertise the
new slice, add the slice + QoS to the UE's subscription **via the WebUI**, edit `ue.yaml` to
request the new slice, restart and verify from logs, an `iperf3` bandwidth comparison between
slices, and a `tshark` capture of the NAS QoS IEs in the PDU Session Establishment Accept.
**There is no bulk-subscriber-dump step anywhere in the guide.** This confirms rather than
merely suggests the recast: `TR-DB-002` in the current catalogue correctly describes the
`mongosh` session as standard subscriber-management tooling whose bulk-read capability is
indistinguishable on the wire from exfiltration, not as a designed extraction attack.

### 2c. `TR-N3-002` — possible false positive *(module1)*

**We said:** user-plane desynchronisation, 352 GTP-U G-PDUs against 352 Error Indications.

**Ground truth:** module 01 is *ping and basic communication* — a benign baseline. Its lecture
audio is literally `4. Debug_Empty_GTP-U_Tunnels_with_SCTP.m4a`, so empty GTP-U tunnels are a
**taught debugging topic**, i.e. expected lab behaviour.

**Verdict:** likely normal-for-this-lab, not an attack signature. Needs re-examination.

### 2d. Module 06 — probable miss

**We said:** for module6, an ARP storm (96,285 requests, 0 replies). Nothing about identity.

**Ground truth:** *Cascading Effects using Network Slice Propagation; Session Hijacking using
IMSI spoofing and Impersonation* — an 18.8 KB lab guide, the largest in the course.

**Verdict:** we almost certainly missed the IMSI spoofing and session hijacking entirely, and
again reported the ARP volume as the finding. This is a **false negative**, which is more
damaging to a reconstruction claim than a false positive.

### 2e. Confirmed

- **`TR-N2-001`** NGAP replay flood *(module3)* — module 03 is *custom replay attack*. Our
  5GReplay attribution and the 69 flows of exactly 4,000 packets stand. ✅
- **`TR-VIRT-001`** container escape *(module7)* — module 07 is *Docker and Host Escape*. ✅
- **`TR-AI-001/002/003`** *(modules 08–09)* — poisoning and robustness confirmed as designed
  content. ⚠️ One detail to check: the course README describes a **Decision Tree** with
  `max_depth` and `class_weight='balanced'`, ASR dropping **55.3% → 2.1%** at 50% poisoning,
  whereas our entries cite `RandomForestClassifier(n_estimators=10, class_weight='balanced')`.
  There are two Module 09 lab guides plus a notebook, so both may be present — but verify.

### 2f. Provisional score

Of the ten modules, three are confirmed, four look wrong or incomplete, and three are not yet
checked. **That is roughly a 40–60% reconstruction accuracy on cause**, against near-100% on
*observation* — we saw the traffic correctly and interpreted its purpose incorrectly.

That gap is the paper. "Passive telemetry reliably reveals *that* something happened and
unreliably reveals *why*" is a real, measurable, defensible finding, and it is exactly what
`NOVELTY-ASSESSMENT.md` §4a predicted would be the contribution.

## 3. Access constraints

| Asset | Status |
|---|---|
| `.json` lab guides | **Readable.** Plain text, 6–19 KB each, fetched over HTTPS |
| `README.md`, Module 09 `.ipynb` | **Readable** (4.3 KB / 19.2 KB) |
| `.docx` books, `.m4a` audio, `.png` diagrams | **Git LFS pointers, 131–132 bytes.** Not retrievable without the LFS client |
| `Module 09/Exercise_Dataset/exercise.csv` (~87 MB) | LFS pointer, 133 bytes |
| `Module 09.7z` | LFS pointer, 133 bytes |

`git clone` fails in this sandbox with a TLS handshake error — this coding session is closed, so
outbound git to GitHub is blocked. Plain HTTPS document fetches still work, which is how the
above was read. **To get the LFS content you need to clone locally yourself** with
`git lfs install && git clone … && git lfs pull`.

Lab guides confirmed present for modules **01, 02, 05, 06, 07, 08, 09** (two for 09). Guides for
00, 03 and 04 were not observed in the portion of the tree inspected — confirm when you clone.

## 4. ⚠️ Copyright — do not vendor this content

The course repository's own README states:

> These are **licensed course materials** (book chapters and recorded lectures) owned by their
> respective rights holders. This repository is published for study and reference only and is
> **not a redistribution channel**. The lectures and books remain under copyright; no licence to
> redistribute them is granted by this repository.

Consequences:

1. **Do not copy the `.json`, `.docx` or `.m4a` content into `threat-repository/`.** Reference
   it, derive from it, quote minimally under fair dealing for criticism and review — but the
   threat repository must not become a second distribution point.
2. **A journal paper needs cleared rights.** Establish who the rights holders are and get
   written permission to quote lab steps and cite the materials. Facts — "module 02 ran 1,000
   UEs against the AMF" — are not copyrightable; the expression is.
3. **Artifact release gets harder.** If the reconstruction is validated against material that
   cannot be redistributed, reviewers cannot check it. Options: publish only the derived
   module→attack mapping and your own labels, or obtain a licence.
4. The notice invites rights holders to request takedown, so the source may not be permanently
   available. **Archive what you need now**, privately.
