\twocolumn[
{\centering\LARGE\bfseries What the Telemetry Knew: Reconstructing a Multi-Stage 5G-IoT Attack Campaign from Retained Evidence, and the Observability Limits It Reveals\par}
\vspace{0.5cm}
{\centering\large [Author Name]$^{1}$\par}
{\centering\small $^1$[Department, Institution, City, Country] --- Corresponding author: [email@domain]\par}
\vspace{0.5cm}
\noindent\rule{\textwidth}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{0.25cm}
\noindent\textbf{Abstract}\par
\noindent
Security datasets for fifth-generation (5G) core networks are almost always built and labelled
by the team that also designs the attacks, so ground truth is available by construction. A real
analyst is never in that position: telemetry arrives after the fact, its provenance is unknown,
and there is no oracle to check a hypothesis against. This paper asks what a passive analyst can
actually recover under those conditions. We take an independently authored, eleven-stage 5G-IoT
red-team exercise --- a containerised Open5GS core, a UERANSIM radio-access simulator, a shared
subscriber database, host-level monitoring (Zabbix/Wazuh), and a machine-learning-based intrusion
detection pipeline --- and reconstruct its attack campaign using only the telemetry a real
operator would retain: nine packet captures, ten NetFlow feature sets and two operator shell
histories. The reconstruction is expressed as 27 confidence-scored findings and mapped against
three MITRE frameworks (ATT\&CK, ATLAS and FiGHT; 84 techniques, 143 mappings), then checked
against the exercise's own published lab guides as an independent ground truth. The cross-check
shows a sharp asymmetry: passive telemetry correctly reveals \emph{that} an attack occurred in
almost every case, but reliably explains \emph{why} in only a minority of the cases we were able
to check independently, and for six of ten designed modules the 5G control-plane protocols
(SCTP, NGAP, GTP-U) that should evidence the exercise's own designed attacks are entirely absent
from the capture. We further show that the platform's own commercial monitoring stack is
dominated by unrelated management traffic and that a representative three-feature ML intrusion
detector is trivially evaded and poisoned. The result is a quantified, reproducible account of
what retained 5G-IoT telemetry can and cannot tell a defender, and a public, machine-readable
artifact (findings catalogue, MITRE mappings and analysis code) for others to extend.
\par\vspace{0.3cm}
\noindent\textbf{Keywords:} 5G core security; IoT security; MITRE ATT\&CK; MITRE FiGHT; MITRE
ATLAS; network forensics; telemetry observability; intrusion detection evasion
\par\vspace{0.15cm}
\noindent\rule{\textwidth}{0.4pt}
\vspace{0.5cm}
]

\section{Introduction}
\label{sec:intro}

Fifth-generation (5G) networks are the connective substrate of most contemporary Internet-of-Things
(IoT) deployments: industrial sensors, connected vehicles and consumer devices attach through a
5G radio-access network (RAN) to a service-based core that authenticates them, admits their
traffic, and hands it to the applications behind it. That core is itself a distributed system of
containerised network functions communicating over interfaces --- N2, N3, N4 and the service-based
interface (SBI) --- that were specified for interoperability first and defence-in-depth second.
Understanding how such a system fails in practice, and what a defender could actually have known
about it, matters more than understanding it in the abstract, because the answer determines what
telemetry is worth collecting at all.

\subsection{Problem statement}

Almost every published 5G security dataset shares one property that quietly limits what it can
teach a reader: the team that built the dataset also designed and executed the attacks, so ground
truth is available by construction. 5G-NIDD states this candidly --- ``we control the attacker
generating the DoS traffic and therefore have the ground-truth knowledge about when the attack is
executed and which attack types'' (Siriwardhana et al., 2025) --- and the same is true, implicitly
or explicitly, of the PFCP-flood testbed of Amponis et al. (2022), the FiGHT-mapped testbed of
Attieh et al. (2024), and most of the wider literature surveyed in Section~\ref{sec:related}.
That is a reasonable way to build a labelled dataset, but it describes a situation no working
analyst is ever actually in. A real security team receives packet captures, flow records and
operator shell histories \emph{after} an incident, with no privileged knowledge of what was
attempted, when, or by whom. The research question this paper asks is therefore not ``can we
build another labelled 5G attack dataset'' but:

\begin{quote}
\emph{Given only the telemetry a real operator would retain, how much of a multi-stage 5G-IoT
attack campaign can be reconstructed, at what confidence, and what is systematically
unrecoverable?}
\end{quote}

We answer it using the NITRO 5G Exercise Dataset v1.0, the packet-capture and shell-history
artifact released alongside the NITRO interconnected 5G-IoT cyber range (Farao et al., 2024). The
exercise was designed and executed by a team we had no part of and no access to; we received only
its retained telemetry, exactly as a forensic responder would. This inversion of the usual
dataset-authorship relationship is the paper's central methodological device, and it is what lets
us measure --- rather than assume --- how faithfully passive observation reconstructs an attacker's
intent.

\subsection{Contributions}

This paper makes five contributions.

\begin{enumerate}
\item An evidential-reconstruction methodology that converts nine packet captures, ten NetFlow
feature sets and two operator shell histories from an independently authored 5G-IoT exercise into
27 confidence-scored security findings, each carrying an explicit evidence class (packet, flow,
shell-history, or derived) and confidence label (Confirmed, Probable or Possible).
\item A ground-truth cross-check of that reconstruction against the exercise's own published lab
guides, functioning as an independent oracle. The cross-check is, to our knowledge, the first
published falsification pass of a 5G security reconstruction against the attacker's own script,
and it yields a measured, not asserted, accuracy figure together with four documented case
studies where the initial reconstruction was wrong or incomplete.
\item A quantified telemetry-sufficiency measurement: protocol presence versus designed activity
across all ten exercise modules, showing that the 5G control-plane protocols that should evidence
three of the exercise's own designed attacks are entirely or partially absent from six of the ten
captures.
\item A tri-framework threat mapping spanning both the network and the AI/ML layers of a converged
5G-IoT deployment --- MITRE ATT\&CK Enterprise, MITRE ATLAS and MITRE FiGHT --- comprising 84
distinct techniques and 143 finding-to-technique mappings, released as a public, machine-readable
artifact together with the underlying findings catalogue.
\item An end-to-end demonstration that neither the exercise platform's own commercial monitoring
stack (Zabbix and Wazuh) nor a representative three-feature machine-learning intrusion detector
observes or resists the reconstructed campaign, closing the loop from ``what happened'' to
``would any deployed control have noticed.''
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Related work}
\label{sec:related}

Public 5G security datasets cluster around network functions exposed to a controlled attacker.
Amponis et al. (2022) build and release a PFCP-based denial-of-service dataset against a 5G core
serving unmanned aerial vehicles, with the attacker and the ground truth under the authors'
control throughout. Attieh et al. (2024) construct a FiGHT-mapped testbed and explicitly frame
threat hunting as the exercise of matching known-injected techniques back to the framework,
which is the mirror image of the present paper's blind reconstruction. Siriwardhana et al.
(2025) release 5G-NIDD, a labelled network-intrusion dataset built the same way, and are unusually
transparent about the implication for ground truth quoted above. Zhang et al. (2025) instead
attack the GTP-U/GTP-C protocol stack itself across seventeen commercial and open-source
implementations, finding systemic parsing and validation failures; their contribution is
protocol-level vulnerability discovery rather than telemetry reconstruction, and their findings
support several of the confidentiality and integrity weaknesses this paper measures on N3/N4
independently. Vanderveen (2022) and Ramezanpour et al. (2023) survey the 5G/6G threat surface at
the specification level, cataloguing weaknesses without an accompanying live testbed; Gong et al.
(2025) similarly survey attacks on 5G standalone deployments. None of this literature reports a
case where the reconstruction was checked against, and found to disagree with, an independent
account of what the attacker actually did --- which is the gap Section~\ref{sec:eval} addresses.

Two supporting bodies of work matter for method rather than findings. Draper-Gil et al. (2016)
introduce the flow-feature methodology this paper's NetFlow evidence class depends on
(implemented here via CICFlowMeter-derived features). Jarkas et al. (2025) survey container
security exploits and defences; the container-escape and privilege findings in
Section~\ref{sec:eval} are specific, evidenced instances of the general failure classes that
survey catalogues. The NITRO cyber range itself (Farao et al., 2024) is the platform this paper
analyses, not a source of security findings in its own right --- to our knowledge this is the
first independent security analysis of telemetry captured on that platform.

\subsection{Paper structure}

Section~\ref{sec:method} describes the taxonomy, evidence classes and cross-check protocol used
to build and validate the findings catalogue. Section~\ref{sec:impl} describes the exercise
architecture, the capture vantage point, and the data-processing pipeline that turns raw
telemetry into catalogue entries. Section~\ref{sec:eval} presents the results: the findings
catalogue itself, the tri-framework mapping, the protocol-presence measurement, and the
ground-truth cross-check. Section~\ref{sec:discussion} discusses what the asymmetry between
occurrence-detection and cause-attribution means for 5G-IoT operators and for dataset-based
security research more broadly, and states the study's limitations plainly.
Section~\ref{sec:conclusion} concludes and outlines future work.

\section{Methodology}
\label{sec:method}

\subsection{Data source and scope}

The NITRO 5G Exercise Dataset v1.0 records an eleven-stage instructional red-team exercise
executed against a containerised, standalone (SA) 5G core built on Open5GS, with UERANSIM
simulating the radio-access network and user equipment, MongoDB as the subscriber data
repository, Zabbix and Wazuh as host-level monitoring, and a Jupyter-based pipeline for training
and attacking a NetFlow-driven intrusion-detection model. The retained artifacts are nine packet
captures (a tenth, module 3, exceeds GitHub's file-size limit and is represented only by its
NetFlow derivative), ten NetFlow feature-set exports totalling 24{,}875 flow records, and two
chronologically overlapping operator bash histories totalling 989 commands. No live network,
production subscriber data, or human-subjects data of any kind is involved: all subscriber
identifiers are synthetic values provisioned for the exercise, and the exercise itself is an
authorised educational activity on isolated infrastructure. This distinguishes the present work
from research involving human participants and places it outside any human-subjects review
requirement.

\subsection{Taxonomy}

Every finding is classified along four independent axes, defined in full in the project's
taxonomy specification and summarised here.

\textbf{Domain.} Nine domains partition the attack surface by 3GPP reference point or
architectural layer: RAN (radio access and radio-link simulation), N2 (NGAP control plane), N3
(GTP-U user plane), N4 (PFCP session management), SUB (subscriber identity and credential
privacy), DB (the subscriber data repository), VIRT (virtualisation, containers and network
segmentation), AI (the security of the ML-based intrusion-detection pipeline), and OPS
(operational security and detection posture).

\textbf{Severity.} Critical, High, Medium and Low, assigned by a fixed rubric that considers
confidentiality/integrity/availability impact and the precondition cost to an attacker (e.g. an
unauthenticated read of production key material is Critical regardless of how it was reached;
loss of a single non-privileged session is Medium at most).

\textbf{Confidence.} Confirmed, Probable or Possible. Confirmed requires positive, first-class
evidence of both the technique and its effect (e.g. the request and the response are both
observed). Probable allows the technique to be positively evidenced while its effect is inferred
from a proxy signal. Possible is reserved for findings supported only by circumstantial or
single-source evidence with a plausible alternative explanation.

\textbf{Evidence class.} Packet (pcap), flow (NetFlow), shell-history, or derived (an inference
that combines two or more of the above, such as attributing an IP address to a network-function
role via its service-based-interface traffic rather than a deployment manifest). Recording the
evidence class per finding is what makes the telemetry-sufficiency measurement in
Section~\ref{sec:eval} possible: it is the raw material the protocol-presence analysis
aggregates.

\subsection{Reconstruction process}

Each finding follows a fixed entry template: a title, domain, severity, confidence, the specific
network assets involved (by IP address and role), a narrative of what happened supported by
frame numbers, flow identifiers or shell-history line numbers, the operational impact, a
detection recommendation, and a mitigation. Network-function role attribution --- which IP address
is the AMF, which is the UPF, which is the subscriber database --- was performed by cross-referencing
JSON payloads on the service-based interface, TCP/SCTP connection graphs, and transport-layer
behaviour (e.g. the host answering PFCP on N4 and holding TCP/27017 open is the UPF and its
adjacent database dependency respectively), not from a deployment manifest that a passive
observer would not have. This is itself a form of evidential reconstruction under uncertainty,
and its known failure mode --- container address reuse across restarts silently invalidating an
IP-to-role binding established earlier in the timeline --- is catalogued as a finding in its own
right (TR-VIRT-005) rather than corrected away.

\subsection{Ground-truth cross-check protocol}
\label{sec:crosscheck-method}

A companion, publicly hosted course repository for the same exercise publishes per-module lab
guides describing the intended attack, its steps, and its declared success condition. These
guides were never consulted while the initial reconstruction was built; they were retrieved
afterwards specifically to serve as an independent check, functioning as the ``authored attack
script'' that every dataset paper discussed in Section~\ref{sec:related} has by construction and
a blind reconstruction does not. For each of the ten designed modules we compared the
reconstruction's claimed cause against the guide's stated objective and steps, and recorded one of
three outcomes: \emph{confirmed} (the reconstruction's technique and cause match the guide),
\emph{revised} (the reconstruction observed a real signal but attributed it to the wrong cause,
requiring the finding to be corrected), or \emph{not independently checked} (no guide-level
comparison was performed for that module, either because course-repository access constraints
prevented it or because the module's designed activity is host-level and outside the scope of
the pcap/NetFlow comparison). Four case studies from this cross-check, together with the
resulting corrections, are reported in Section~\ref{sec:eval-crosscheck}. We report this
process's coverage honestly in Section~\ref{sec:limitations}: it reached seven of the ten modules
and a minority of the 27 catalogue entries, not an exhaustive per-finding audit.

\subsection{Threat mapping methodology}

Each finding is mapped to one or more techniques in MITRE ATT\&CK Enterprise, MITRE ATLAS, or
MITRE FiGHT, following a most-specific-framework-wins rule: a 5G-specific FiGHT technique (e.g.
\texttt{FGT5031} Discover Tunnel Endpoint ID) is preferred over a generic ATT\&CK parent (e.g.
\texttt{T1046} Network Service Discovery) whenever one exists and applies; ATT\&CK is used where
the behaviour is generically an IT/network technique with no 5G-specific analogue; ATLAS is used
exclusively for the AI/ML-pipeline findings. Where a FiGHT technique is explicitly marked upstream
as an adaptation of an ATT\&CK parent, both are cited, since the parent frequently adds context
the child omits. All technique identifiers were verified directly against the three frameworks'
current public definitions at the time of writing rather than recalled from secondary literature,
which matters in practice: MITRE ATLAS renamed several techniques it inherited from its
predecessor namespace (e.g. \texttt{AML.T0020} is now \emph{Training Data Poisoning}, not
\emph{Poison Training Data}), and citing the old names would misrepresent the current framework.

\section{Implementation and Mechanics}
\label{sec:impl}

\subsection{Exercise architecture and capture vantage point}

Figure~\ref{fig:architecture} shows the reconstructed architecture of the analysed environment.
User equipment (simulated by UERANSIM) attaches over a simulated radio link to a gNB, which
connects over N2/N3 to the Open5GS core's control-plane and user-plane functions (AMF, SMF, UPF),
which in turn depend on the NRF and SCP for service discovery and on the AUSF, PCF, UDM and UDR
for authentication, policy and subscriber-data mediation; the UDR's backing store is a MongoDB
instance. Zabbix and Wazuh provide host-level monitoring, and a separate ML-based
intrusion-detection pipeline consumes NetFlow-style features derived from the same traffic. The
critical architectural property, evidenced independently by an ARP census across every capture in
the corpus, is that every one of these components --- 3GPP network functions, the subscriber
database, the RAN simulator, and the monitoring stack alike --- shares a single, unsegmented
Docker bridge network (TR-VIRT-004, dashed boundary in Figure~\ref{fig:architecture}). There is no
policy boundary an attacker must cross to reach any interface from any other; the ``attacker must
first reach interface X'' precondition that makes many 5G specification-level weaknesses
theoretical is satisfied by default for every workload on the host.

Telemetry was collected from a single vantage point: a host-wide \texttt{tcpdump} process spanning
all interfaces (TR-OPS-001, annotated in Figure~\ref{fig:architecture}), invoked and torn down
around each module by the operator's own shell commands (\texttt{sudo nohup tcpdump -i any -w
moduleN.pcap ... \&} paired with a matching \texttt{sudo kill -15}), which is what allows commands
in the bash history to be attributed to a specific capture window. A full-fidelity, single-host
vantage point is both a strength (nothing on the wire between containers is structurally
invisible to it) and a limitation (traffic on a second Docker network the host does not bridge to
is invisible regardless of fidelity) --- a limitation that becomes concrete in
Section~\ref{sec:eval-crosscheck}.

\begin{figure*}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.85\textwidth]{figures/fig_architecture.png}
\caption{Reconstructed range architecture. Simulated UEs attach through a gNB to the Open5GS
control- and user-plane functions, which depend on NRF/SCP for discovery and on
AUSF/PCF/UDM/UDR (backed by MongoDB) for authentication and subscriber data. Zabbix/Wazuh and an
ML-based IDS observe the same environment. The dashed boundary marks the single, unsegmented
Docker bridge network that every component shares (TR-VIRT-004); the annotated arrow marks the
single host-wide capture vantage point (TR-OPS-001).}
\label{fig:architecture}
\end{figure*}

\subsection{Per-module capture summary}

Table~\ref{tab:modules} summarises what each of the ten designed exercise modules recorded: its
approximate UTC capture window, its frame count, a short description of what the telemetry shows,
and the catalogue findings first evidenced in that window. This table is the raw material for
both the findings catalogue (Section~\ref{sec:eval-catalogue}) and the telemetry-sufficiency
measurement (Section~\ref{sec:eval-telemetry}): every frame count in Figure~\ref{fig:timeline} and
every protocol count in Table~\ref{tab:protocol} is drawn directly from these ten captures and
their NetFlow derivatives.

\begin{table*}[t]
\centering
\caption{Per-module capture summary. Module 3's packet capture exceeds the hosting platform's
file-size limit and is represented only by its NetFlow derivative; all other modules retain a
full packet capture.}
\label{tab:modules}
\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.7cm} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.5cm} >{\raggedleft\arraybackslash}p{1.5cm} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{7.5cm} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.6cm}@{}}
\hline
\textbf{Mod.} & \textbf{Window (UTC)} & \textbf{Frames} & \textbf{What the telemetry shows} & \textbf{Findings first evidenced} \\
\hline
0 & 26 Aug 20:23--21:04 & 107,451 & Core brought up, subscribers registered, first UE attach; an ephemeral container queries the subscriber database. & TR-DB-001, TR-DB-002, TR-SUB-002 \\
1 & 27 Aug 19:19--20:06 & 131,027 & PDU sessions established; ICMP pushed through the GTP-U tunnel; TEIDs enumerated; every G-PDU answered with an Error Indication. & TR-N3-001, TR-N3-002, TR-N4-001, TR-SUB-001, TR-SUB-003, TR-SUB-004 \\

8 & 9 Sep 22:53--23:11 & 59,699 & IDS training corpus built with \texttt{tshark}; clean model trained; poisoned model trained and its Attack Success Rate measured. & TR-AI-001, TR-AI-003, TR-AI-004 \\
9 & 9 Sep 23:25--23:34 & 22,347 & Adversarial evasion of the IDS; adversarial training and an ensemble countermeasure evaluated. & TR-AI-002 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\vspace{0.1cm}
\raggedright\footnotesize $^{\dagger}$Frame count for module 3 is derived from its NetFlow export; the raw packet capture itself is excluded from the public repository for size reasons (581.7\,MB, exceeding the hosting platform's per-file limit).
\end{table*}

\subsection{Data-processing pipeline}

Raw telemetry was processed in three stages. First, packet captures were parsed protocol-by-protocol
(SCTP/NGAP, GTP-U, PFCP, HTTP/2 SBI traffic, ARP, MongoDB wire protocol) to extract the specific
frames, procedures and payload fields cited as evidence in each finding. Second, the NetFlow
feature exports (24{,}875 flow records across the ten modules) were aggregated by source/destination
pair and destination port to compute the per-module protocol-presence counts in
Table~\ref{tab:protocol} and to identify anomalous flow concentrations, such as the Zabbix
trapper-port dominance discussed in Section~\ref{sec:eval-monitoring}. Third, the two operator
bash histories were time-aligned against the packet-capture invocation and teardown commands
described in Section~\ref{sec:impl}, which is what allows a shell command with no corresponding
network evidence (for example, the container-escape sequence in module 7) to be attributed with
confidence to a specific point in the timeline despite being invisible on the wire by
construction. Findings whose only possible evidence is host-local activity of this kind are
still classified with the full confidence rubric of Section~\ref{sec:method}; the correct
Confirmed rating for TR-VIRT-001 rests on shell-history evidence alone, and that is by design, not
a gap.

\section{Evaluation}
\label{sec:eval}

This section reports four sets of results in turn: the findings catalogue itself
(Section~\ref{sec:eval-catalogue}), the tri-framework threat mapping
(Section~\ref{sec:eval-mapping}), the telemetry-sufficiency measurement
(Section~\ref{sec:eval-telemetry}), and the ground-truth cross-check
(Section~\ref{sec:eval-crosscheck}), closing with the monitoring and machine-learning detection
results (Section~\ref{sec:eval-monitoring}).

\subsection{The findings catalogue}
\label{sec:eval-catalogue}

The reconstruction yields 27 findings: 8 Critical, 15 High and 4 Medium severity, of which 24 are
Confirmed and 3 are Probable (Figure~\ref{fig:sevconf}). No finding was rated Low or Possible;
every finding that survived triage was supported by at least a proxy signal for its effect, and
none of the environment's weaknesses were judged low-consequence given the shared, unsegmented
network described in Section~\ref{sec:impl}. Table~\ref{tab:domains} breaks this down by domain.
Virtualisation and segmentation (VIRT) carries the most findings (5, all Confirmed), reflecting
that container-boundary and network-segmentation weaknesses are both easy to observe directly and
consequential once observed. The AI/ML domain carries four High-or-Medium findings, all Confirmed,
establishing that the intrusion-detection layer is not a hardened backstop for the weaknesses
found elsewhere but an additional attack surface in its own right.

\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r}
\hline
\textbf{Dom.} & \textbf{N} & \textbf{Crit.} & \textbf{High} & \textbf{Med.} & \textbf{Conf.(Prob.)} \\
\hline
RAN & 3 & 1 & 2 & 0 & 1 (2) \\
N2  & 2 & 1 & 1 & 0 & 2 (0) \\
N3  & 2 & 0 & 2 & 0 & 2 (0) \\
N4  & 3 & 2 & 1 & 0 & 2 (1) \\
SUB & 4 & 1 & 3 & 0 & 4 (0) \\
DB  & 2 & 1 & 1 & 0 & 2 (0) \\
VIRT& 5 & 2 & 2 & 1 & 5 (0) \\
AI  & 4 & 0 & 3 & 1 & 4 (0) \\
OPS & 2 & 0 & 0 & 2 & 2 (0) \\
\hline

\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

and 24 are rated Confirmed; no finding is rated Low or Possible.}

\end{figure}

Figure~\ref{fig:radar} presents the same catalogue as a severity-weighted radar chart per domain
(Critical\,=\,3, High\,=\,2, Medium\,=\,1, summed per domain), which makes the relative weight of
VIRT and SUB visually apparent against the comparatively contained N2/N3/OPS domains --- despite
N2 and N4 each containing the single highest-severity individual findings in the catalogue
(TR-N2-001, TR-N4-001, TR-N4-002).

summed). VIRT and SUB dominate by aggregate weight; N4 and N2 each contain individual Critical
findings despite lower domain totals.}
\label{fig:radar}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Tri-framework threat mapping}
\label{sec:eval-mapping}

The 27 findings map to 84 distinct techniques across the three frameworks and 143 finding-to-technique
mappings in total (Table~\ref{tab:frameworks}, Figure~\ref{fig:frameworks}): 32 ATT\&CK techniques
(63 mappings), 16 ATLAS techniques (19 mappings) and 36 FiGHT techniques (61 mappings). FiGHT
carries the largest technique count despite the smallest per-technique mapping average, consistent
with 5G-specific behaviour (TEID enumeration, SEID space exhaustion, SUCI null-scheme abuse) being
finely subdivided in that framework relative to the coarser generic categories ATT\&CK offers for
the same behaviour class.

\begin{table}[t]
\centering
\caption{Technique coverage by framework.}
\label{tab:frameworks}
\small
\begin{tabularx}{\columnwidth}{l Y r r}
\hline
\textbf{Framework} & \textbf{Scope} & \textbf{Tech.} & \textbf{Map.} \\
\hline
ATT\&CK Enterprise & General IT adversary behaviour & 32 & 63 \\
ATLAS & Adversarial threats to AI systems & 16 & 19 \\
FiGHT & 5G-specific adversary behaviour & 36 & 61 \\
\hline
\textbf{Total} & & \textbf{84} & \textbf{143} \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.98\columnwidth]{figures/fig_framework_coverage.png}
\caption{Techniques cited and finding-to-technique mappings per framework. FiGHT and ATT\&CK
between them account for 124 of 143 mappings; ATLAS, scoped only to the AI/ML pipeline, accounts
for the remainder.}
\label{fig:frameworks}
\end{figure}

Figure~\ref{fig:tactics} reads the same catalogue by ATT\&CK tactic rather than by framework,
showing which stage of a canonical kill chain each domain contributes evidence to. The exercise
traverses nine of eleven enterprise tactics coherently, from Reconnaissance (container and
network enumeration) through Credential Access (the unauthenticated database read), Discovery
(TEID/SEID enumeration), Defense Evasion (IDS poisoning and evasion) to Impact (the NGAP replay
flood). Persistence and Command and Control are deliberately absent: nothing in the dataset shows
an implant, a modified image or a covert channel, and all operator activity arrives over a
legitimate management-network SSH session. Both absences are recorded as an explicit non-finding
rather than an analysis gap, since a false claim of coverage where none exists would undermine the
credibility of the coverage that is genuinely present.

\begin{figure*}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.88\textwidth]{figures/fig_tactic_heatmap.png}
\caption{ATT\&CK tactic coverage by domain. Nine of eleven enterprise tactics are evidenced;
Persistence and Command and Control are absent by design (Section~\ref{sec:eval-mapping}), not
by omission.}
\label{fig:tactics}
\end{figure*}

\subsection{Telemetry sufficiency: protocol presence versus designed activity}
\label{sec:eval-telemetry}

The central empirical question this paper asks --- how much of a designed multi-stage attack survives
in retained telemetry --- is answered directly by counting, per module, the presence of the four
protocol signatures that should evidence a 5G control- or user-plane attack: SCTP associations
(which carry NGAP), NGAP frames themselves, GTP-U packets, and RLS (UERANSIM's simulated radio-link
control channel, port 4997) flows. Table~\ref{tab:protocol} reports these counts against each
module's designed activity, and Figure~\ref{fig:protocol} renders the same data as a heatmap on a
$\log_{10}(\text{count}+1)$ colour scale.

\begin{table*}[t]
\centering
\caption{Protocol presence versus designed activity, per module. Bold designed activities are
5G control- or user-plane attacks that should produce SCTP/NGAP/GTP-U evidence.}
\label{tab:protocol}
\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.7cm} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{4.7cm} >{\raggedleft\arraybackslash}p{1.1cm} >{\raggedleft\arraybackslash}p{1.1cm} >{\raggedleft\arraybackslash}p{1.1cm} >{\raggedleft\arraybackslash}p{1.0cm} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.1cm}@{}}
\hline
\textbf{Mod.} & \textbf{Designed activity} & \textbf{SCTP} & \textbf{NGAP} & \textbf{GTP-U} & \textbf{RLS} & \textbf{Attack visible?} \\
\hline
0 & Introduction / fundamentals & 20 & 48 & 0 & -- & n/a (baseline) \\
1 & Ping and basic communication & 23 & 350 & 704 & -- & Yes \\
2 & \textbf{IMSI flood against the AMF} & 5 & 184 & 0 & 763 & \textbf{No} (below baseline) \\
3 & \textbf{Custom NGAP replay attack} & 150 & flood & -- & -- & Yes \\
4 & \textbf{Reconnaissance} & 0 & 0 & 0 & 0 & Partial (ARP only) \\
5 & Network slice configuration & 0 & 0 & 0 & 603 & Partial (SBI/DB only) \\
6 & \textbf{IMSI spoofing, session hijack} & 0 & 0 & 0 & 0 & \textbf{No} \\
7 & Docker / host escape & 0 & 0 & 0 & 0 & N/a (host-level) \\
8 & NetFlow capture, ML-IDS training & 0 & 0 & 0 & 0 & N/a (host-level) \\
9 & Adversarial AI evasion/defence & 0 & 0 & 0 & 0 & N/a (host-level) \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\par\vspace{0.1cm}
\footnotesize Rows 2 and 6 are discussed in detail in Section~\ref{sec:eval-crosscheck}: the
designed attack's defining protocol signal is below baseline (module 2) or entirely absent
(module 6) despite the attack being confirmed in the exercise's own lab guide.
\end{table*}

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.98\columnwidth]{figures/fig_protocol_heatmap.png}
\caption{Protocol-presence heatmap, $\log_{10}(\text{count}+1)$ scale. SCTP/NGAP/GTP-U exist only
in modules 0--3; from module 4 onward the 5G control plane is entirely absent from the capture.}
\label{fig:protocol}
\end{figure}

The headline result is stark: SCTP and NGAP exist only in modules 0 through 3, and from module 4
onward there is no 5G control-plane evidence in the captures at all --- no SCTP, no NGAP, no
GTP-U. This is a problem specifically because at least three of the six affected modules (2, 6,
and to a lesser extent 5) are designed 5G control-plane attacks. Module 2 is a 1{,}000-simulated-UE
registration flood against the AMF; a NAS registration flood of that scale should produce
thousands of NGAP \texttt{InitialUEMessage} procedures, yet the capture records \emph{fewer} SCTP
flows (5) than either of the two clean baseline modules either side of it (20 and 23). The defining
signal of the designed attack is not merely weak: it is below the benign baseline. What the
capture does hold is the flood's second-order effect --- 103,902 ARP frames and 763 RLS flows
generated as 1{,}000 UERANSIM instances cycle through cell search --- which is why the initial,
pre-cross-check reconstruction attributed the traffic to the wrong cause
(Section~\ref{sec:eval-crosscheck}). Module 6, the largest single lab guide in the exercise and
designed around cross-slice IMSI spoofing and session hijacking, left \emph{no} network evidence
whatsoever: every protocol count is zero. Consistent with the bash histories (repeated
\texttt{docker compose down/up} and container restarts), the most likely explanation is that the
RAN simulator was not attached to the core during the captures for modules 4 through 9; the user
equipment container was sometimes running (module 5 shows 603 RLS flows) but its association to a
gNB was not.

Figure~\ref{fig:timeline} places this in context against overall capture volume: frame counts vary
by two orders of magnitude across modules (22,347 to 4,267,925), and colour-codes each module by
an evidentiary-completeness tier (fully evidenced, partially evidenced, a starved network attack,
or correctly host-level). Modules 0, 1 and 3 are fully evidenced; modules 4, 5 and 6 are
inadequately evidenced for the 5G-signalling attacks they were designed to demonstrate; modules 7,
8 and 9 are correctly absent from network telemetry because their designed activity --- container
escape and Jupyter-based adversarial machine learning --- is host-local by construction, and their
absence from the capture is expected rather than a defect. On this accounting, three of ten
designed modules are fully evidenced by their packet captures, three are partially evidenced, and
four were never going to be visible on the wire regardless of what happened.

\begin{figure*}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{figures/fig_timeline.png}
\caption{Frame count per module ($\log_{10}$ scale) coloured by evidentiary-completeness tier.
Green: fully evidenced by the packet capture. Amber: partially evidenced. Red: a starved 5G
control-plane attack (designed but not observed). Grey: correctly host-level, no network evidence
expected.}
\label{fig:timeline}
\end{figure*}

Figure~\ref{fig:scatter} cross-plots frame count against NetFlow flow count per module on the same
colour scale, which separates the ``high activity, low flow diversity'' signature of the NGAP
replay flood (module 3: highest frame count, comparatively low flow count, consistent with a small
number of connections each carrying enormous packet volume) from the ``high flow diversity''
signature of the module-2 registration flood (comparatively high flow count from many distinct
short-lived UE instances), even though neither module's designed attack is legible in the
protocol-specific counts of Table~\ref{tab:protocol} alone.

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.98\columnwidth]{figures/fig_scatter.png}
\caption{Frame count ($\log_{10}$ scale) versus NetFlow flow count per module, coloured by the
same evidentiary-completeness tier as Figure~\ref{fig:timeline}. Module 3's low flow-count/high
frame-count signature and module 2's elevated flow count are both visible even where the
underlying 5G protocol counts are not.}
\label{fig:scatter}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Ground-truth cross-check}
\label{sec:eval-crosscheck}

Section~\ref{sec:crosscheck-method} described the cross-check protocol; this section reports its
results. Table~\ref{tab:crosscheck} presents four case studies where comparing the initial
reconstruction against the exercise's own lab guide changed the finding.

\begin{table*}[t]
\centering
\caption{Case studies from the ground-truth cross-check.}
\label{tab:crosscheck}
\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.2cm} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{7.3cm} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{7.3cm}@{}}
\hline
\textbf{Finding} & \textbf{Initial reconstruction} & \textbf{Ground truth and resolution} \\
\hline
TR-RAN-002 (module 2) & Stale RAN peer causing an unanswered ARP/RLS storm (101,159 requests, 32 replies). & Guide specifies a 1,000-simulated-UE IMSI-flood registration attack against the AMF (5 parallel processes $\times$ 200 UEs, repeated 2--3 times). The ARP/RLS volume is real but is the flood's side effect, not a stale peer; the finding was recast as an AMF resource-exhaustion attack whose defining NGAP signal is itself absent from the capture (Section~\ref{sec:eval-telemetry}). \\
TR-DB-002 (module 5) & Bulk subscriber-database extraction via an ephemeral container running \texttt{mongosh}. & Guide describes an eight-step network-slice configuration exercise in which adding a slice requires editing subscriber documents in MongoDB via the standard subscriber-management tool. No extraction step exists in the guide. Recast as ``standard subscriber-management access whose bulk-read capability is indistinguishable on the wire from exfiltration'' --- itself a stronger finding than the original. \\
TR-N3-002 (module 1) & User-plane desynchronisation: every G-PDU met with a GTP-U Error Indication. & Module 1 is explicitly a benign ping/communication baseline, and the exercise's own instructional material treats empty GTP-U tunnels as a taught debugging topic. Flagged as likely normal-for-this-lab rather than an attack signature. \\
Module 6 (miss) & An unanswered ARP storm (96,285 requests, 0 replies); no identity-layer finding recorded. & Guide describes cross-slice IMSI spoofing and session hijacking, the largest lab guide in the exercise. The initial pass reported only the ARP side-effect and missed the designed identity attack entirely --- a false negative, added post-cross-check as TR-RAN-003. \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table*}

Beyond these four corrections, the cross-check independently \emph{confirmed} five findings
against their guides without revision: the module-3 NGAP replay attribution (TR-N2-001, matching
69 flows of exactly 4,000 packets each to the guide's described replay procedure), the module-7
container-escape attribution (TR-VIRT-001), and the module-8/9 AI poisoning and evasion
attributions (TR-AI-001, TR-AI-002, TR-AI-003). Counting at the level of the ten designed modules,
the cross-check reached seven of ten (modules 1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 7 and 8/9 jointly); of those seven,
three modules' reconstructions were confirmed as originally stated and four required correction or
addition, for a module-level as-stated accuracy of roughly three in seven ($\approx$43\%) on
\emph{cause} attribution, against effectively 100\% accuracy on \emph{occurrence} --- every module
where something happened was correctly identified as containing an event, and the error was
consistently in explaining what that event was, never in whether one occurred. Modules 0 and 4 were
not independently checked against a lab guide at the time of writing. We treat this gap explicitly
in Section~\ref{sec:limitations}.

\subsection{Monitoring blind spots and machine-learning detection}
\label{sec:eval-monitoring}

Two further results establish that this observability gap is not confined to passive forensic
reconstruction; it also affects the deployed, real-time controls the exercise platform actually
runs. First, aggregating the NetFlow destination-port distribution across all ten modules shows
that TCP/10051 --- the Zabbix trapper port used for host-monitoring data --- is the single busiest
destination port in eight of the ten captures (946 flows in module 0, 2{,}872 in module 2, 2{,}516
in module 6), making the monitoring system the loudest single talker in the entire dataset. Nothing
in the retained telemetry indicates that this monitoring stack raised an alert on any of the 27
findings, which is unsurprising once the traffic distribution is examined: Zabbix and Wazuh are
built to watch hosts, and the attacks in this dataset are overwhelmingly in the 5G control and user
planes, which host-level monitoring was never positioned to see.

Second, the exercise's own ML-based intrusion-detection model --- trained on the same NetFlow corpus
this paper uses as evidence --- is both easy to evade and easy to poison. The deployed classifier
uses three features: protocol number and two packet-length statistics, none of which measures
rate. Since a flood and a benign burst of the same packet size are indistinguishable to a model
with no rate feature, scaling a single packet-length feature by a constant factor evades detection
in roughly three attempts of four (TR-AI-002), and this is not a fixable weakness of one trained
instance but a structural consequence of the three-feature design (TR-AI-003). Separately,
label-flipping a fraction of the training corpus (the same NetFlow data the model would otherwise
learn from) drops detection of the targeted attack class sharply, consistent with an Attack
Success Rate curve reported in the exercise's own instructional material (TR-AI-001). Both results
depend on the same root cause identified for the monitoring stack: the analytics layer, whether
rule-based or learned, was built to characterise traffic that is not the traffic the reconstructed
campaign actually generates.

\section{Discussion}
\label{sec:discussion}

\subsection{Occurrence versus cause: what passive telemetry actually buys a defender}

The central empirical pattern across Sections~\ref{sec:eval-telemetry} and
\ref{sec:eval-crosscheck} is an asymmetry between two different things a reconstruction can get
right. \emph{Occurrence detection} --- noticing that something anomalous happened in a given window
--- was essentially perfect: every module in which the exercise guide describes an attack shows
some anomalous signal in the telemetry, even when that signal is a second-order side effect rather
than the attack's defining behaviour. \emph{Cause attribution} --- correctly explaining what that
anomaly was --- was substantially weaker, correct in roughly three of the seven modules where it
could be checked, and wrong in the specific, informative way of substituting an attack's collateral
signature (an ARP storm) for its actual mechanism (a registration flood, or a cross-slice identity
attack). This is a materially different, and more useful, statement than either ``our detection
worked'' or ``our detection failed'': it says a defender using only this class of retained
telemetry can be confident an incident occurred at a specific time on a specific host pair, and
should not be confident about \emph{why}, without additional first-class evidence (a control-plane
capture that actually contains the attack's defining protocol, not just its side effects). This is
the paper's central, reusable finding, and it survives independently of whether any specific one of
the 27 findings turns out to be revised further on deeper investigation.

\subsection{Implications for 5G-IoT telemetry design}

The protocol-presence measurement in Table~\ref{tab:protocol} has a direct operational reading:
five of nine designed non-baseline modules (2, 4, 5, 6, and arguably the un-flooded portions of 3)
were not adequately evidenced by whatever capture configuration was in effect at the time, despite
a full-fidelity, host-wide vantage point being available in principle. This is not a case for
capturing \emph{more} data in general --- module 3 alone is 581.7\,MB and already strains
repository tooling --- but for capturing the \emph{right} data continuously rather than
intermittently. A RAN simulator that becomes silently detached from the core between exercise
modules, as the evidence in Section~\ref{sec:eval-telemetry} suggests happened here, produces
exactly the false confidence a defender must avoid: telemetry that looks complete (a packet
capture exists, with a plausible frame count) but is missing precisely the signal that would
distinguish a designed attack from ambient noise. For an operational 5G-IoT deployment, the
practical implication is that control-plane visibility (SCTP/NGAP on N2, GTP-U on N3, PFCP on N4)
needs to be verified as continuously present, not merely provisioned, since its absence is silent
and produces telemetry that is superficially indistinguishable from a healthy capture.

\subsection{Implications for dataset-based 5G security research}

Section~\ref{sec:related} noted that essentially all published 5G attack datasets are built by
the same team that designs the attacks, which gives them ground truth by construction and no
opportunity to observe reconstruction failure. This paper's inversion --- analysing telemetry from
an exercise we did not design --- is what makes the occurrence/cause asymmetry of
Section~\ref{sec:discussion} visible at all: a dataset paper with authorial ground truth has no
mechanism by which a wrong cause attribution could ever surface, because the authors already know
the cause and label accordingly. This suggests a methodological recommendation for the field:
published 5G-security datasets would benefit from an independent, blind reconstruction pass by a
team without access to the attack script, with the resulting agreement or disagreement reported
alongside the dataset itself, exactly as Section~\ref{sec:eval-crosscheck} reports it here. Doing
so converts an assumed labelling accuracy into a measured one, which is a stronger and more
citable claim.

\subsection{Limitations}
\label{sec:limitations}

Four limitations bound how far these results generalise. First, the ground-truth cross-check
(Section~\ref{sec:eval-crosscheck}) reached seven of the ten designed modules and, by extension, a
minority of the 27 catalogue findings directly; the remaining findings --- including those built on
modules 0 and 4 --- are internally consistent with their supporting telemetry but have not been
independently verified against the exercise's own script, and should be read with that caveat.
This is disclosed rather than smoothed over deliberately: an unqualified accuracy claim over all
27 findings would not be supportable from the evidence actually gathered. Second, the analysis
uses a single capture vantage point; a workload on a Docker network the host does not bridge to
(such as the second gNB discovered in module 6, on a subnet the primary capture never records
regardless of its exact address) is invisible no matter how complete the capture at the observed
vantage point is, which is a structural, not an incidental, limitation of single-host monitoring.
Third, network-function role attribution by IP address (Section~\ref{sec:impl}) degrades under
container address reuse across restarts, a failure mode this paper catalogues as a finding
(TR-VIRT-005) precisely because it was observed to occur, not merely hypothesised. Fourth, the
telemetry-sufficiency and monitoring results in Sections~\ref{sec:eval-telemetry} and
\ref{sec:eval-monitoring} describe one testbed configuration at one point in time; we do not claim
these exact protocol-presence counts generalise to other 5G-core deployments, only that the
methodology used to measure them --- comparing protocol presence against independently documented
designed activity --- is deployment-agnostic and directly reusable.

\section{Conclusion}
\label{sec:conclusion}

This paper reconstructed a multi-stage 5G-IoT attack campaign from the telemetry a real operator
would retain --- packet captures, NetFlow records and shell histories --- without access to the
exercise's own design or intent, and then measured that reconstruction against an independent
ground truth rather than merely asserting its accuracy. The result is a catalogue of 27
confidence-scored findings mapped to 84 techniques across MITRE ATT\&CK, ATLAS and FiGHT; a
quantified demonstration that the 5G control-plane telemetry needed to evidence a designed attack
was entirely or partially absent in six of ten exercise modules; a documented, four-case
falsification pass showing that passive reconstruction correctly detects that an incident occurred
far more reliably than it explains why; and a demonstration that neither the platform's commercial
monitoring stack nor a representative machine-learning intrusion detector observes or resists the
campaign this paper reconstructs. Together these results argue that telemetry sufficiency, not
just telemetry volume, is the binding constraint on what a 5G-IoT operator can actually know about
an incident, and that claims of reconstruction accuracy in this domain --- including the reconstruction
in this paper --- should be measured against an independent oracle wherever one is available,
rather than assumed.

Future work falls into three areas. First, extending the ground-truth cross-check to the three
modules not yet independently verified (Section~\ref{sec:limitations}) would close the paper's
largest disclosed gap. Second, a controlled re-run of the modules found to be under-captured
(2, 4, 5 and 6) with corrected, continuously verified control-plane capture would allow a direct,
paired comparison between what this paper recovered from the original telemetry and what a
correctly instrumented capture would have shown, isolating how much of the observed
occurrence/cause gap is attributable to capture configuration versus to the inherent difficulty of
attribution from passive telemetry alone. Third, the three-feature intrusion-detection weaknesses
identified in Section~\ref{sec:eval-monitoring} motivate a follow-up evaluation of rate-aware and
adversarially trained detectors on the same corpus, to establish whether the evasion and poisoning
results reported here are a property of this specific model or of NetFlow-only 5G intrusion
detection more generally. The findings catalogue, MITRE mappings and figure-generation code
underlying this paper are released publicly to support all three directions.

\section*{Declaration of Competing Interest}
The authors declare that they have no known competing financial interests or personal
relationships that could have appeared to influence the work reported in this paper.

\section*{Ethical Statement}
This study involves no human subjects and no personally identifiable information. All analysed
telemetry originates from an authorised, isolated educational cyber-range exercise; all subscriber
identifiers, credentials and network addresses are synthetic values provisioned for that exercise
and do not correspond to real individuals, devices or operator infrastructure.

\section*{Data Availability}
The findings catalogue, MITRE ATT\&CK/ATLAS/FiGHT mapping tables, methodology documentation and
figure-generation code are available in the corresponding public code repository. The underlying
NITRO 5G Exercise Dataset v1.0 packet captures, NetFlow exports and shell histories are third-party
artifacts published separately by their original authors; access constraints on portions of the
companion course material are documented in the repository's ground-truth cross-check notes.

threat-repository/ENVIRONMENT.md

# Environment

The asset inventory and topology of the NITRO exercise range, reconstructed entirely from
captured traffic. Container names in the dataset are pseudonymised into unreadable strings, so
nothing here is taken from a label — every role is derived from behaviour, and each attribution
records the evidence that produced it.

---

## 1. The two networks

| Network | Range | What it carries |
|---|---|---|
| **5G core fabric** | `172.18.0.0/16` (Docker bridge) | Every 3GPP reference point — N1/N2, N3, N4, and the whole service-based interface — plus the subscriber database, on a single flat L2 segment. |
| **Management / enterprise** | `10.10.0.0/16` | Operator SSH, DNS, Zabbix monitoring, Wazuh logging. The container host sits on both. |

The single most consequential structural fact in this dataset is that the first row has **no
internal segmentation**. N2 signalling, N3 user traffic, N4 session management, SBI service calls
and unauthenticated MongoDB all share one broadcast domain. 3GPP TS 33.501 assumes these are
separable trust domains; here they are not. This is catalogued as **TR-VIRT-004** and it is the
enabling condition for roughly half of the other findings.

**A third network exists but was never captured.** `bash_history_v2` (`arp-scan --localnet
--interface eth0`, `TR-RAN-003`) confirms a second gNB was located, serving a second network
slice on what is necessarily a third Docker network, separate from both rows above — the actual
command output is not preserved in the shell history (only the invocation itself is), so its
exact address is not directly observable here. The course guide this exercise follows states its
own worked-example answer as `172.19.0.18`, i.e. a `172.19.0.0/16` block; that specific value
should be read as the guide's canonical answer rather than an independently confirmed fact about
this run (see `TR-RAN-003`). What *is* confirmed regardless of the exact address: no capture in
this dataset includes a host from any address outside `172.18.0.0/16` or `10.10.0.0/16` — the
second slice's network is known to exist purely from shell-history evidence, never from traffic.
This is not a gap in this document's methodology; it is the fact the methodology is built to
surface. Anyone re-running these exercises (see `rerun-package/`) should capture this network
explicitly — it is capture fix #2 in `rerun-package/README.md`.

---

## 2. 5G core assets (`172.18.0.0/16`)

| Address | Role | How it was identified |
|---|---|---|
| `172.18.0.1` | Docker bridge gateway / container host | Source of the NGAP replay injection in module3; not a 5G function. |
| `172.18.0.2` | **MongoDB** — UDR backing store | TCP/27017; responses contain `open5gs.subscribers`, `admin.$cmd`, and BSON subscriber documents. |
| `172.18.0.3` | Network function (SBI client, role unresolved) | Registers with the SCP on 7777 but emits no distinguishing service payload. Candidate: BSF or NSSF. |
| `172.18.0.4` | **UDR** | Returns `{"authenticationMethod":"5G_AKA","encPermanentKey":...,"encOpcKey":...}` and queries `open5gs.subscribers` on MongoDB directly. |
| `172.18.0.5` | **WebUI / provisioning front end** | Sustained TCP to `172.18.0.2:27017`, no SBI service role, no NF registration payload. |
| `172.18.0.6` | **AUSF** | Emits `{"servingNetworkName":"...","ausfInstanceId":"e3860042-9746-41f1-acee-f5a3b090dc7f"}`. |
| `172.18.0.7` | **UDM** | Receives Nudm_UECM registration: `{"amfInstanceId":"e44528d2-...","pei":"imeisv-...","deregCallbackUri":...}`; returns `subscribedUeAmbr` and `subscribedSnssaiInfos`. |
| `172.18.0.8` | **PCF** | Named in SM policy responses as `{"pcfIpEndPoints":[{"ipv4Address":"172.18.0.8","port":7777}]}`. |
| `172.18.0.9` | **SMF** | `smfInstanceId` `e4266780-9746-41f1-b2f8-d7543eb0bbe1`, callbacks under `/nsmf-callback/v1/`; PFCP peer on UDP/8805. |
| `172.18.0.10` | **SCP** (Service Communication Proxy) | Eight distinct NFs connect *to* it on 7777, and it is the **only** client of the NRF. Indirect-communication model; nothing else fits that graph. |
| `172.18.0.11` | Network function (SBI client, role unresolved) | As `.3`. Candidate: NSSF or BSF. |
| `172.18.0.12` | **UPF** | PFCP peer of the SMF on UDP/8805; GTP-U endpoint on UDP/2152. |
| `172.18.0.13` | **NRF** | Sole SBI server whose only client is the SCP; receives continuous `PATCH [{"op":"replace","path":"/nfStatus","value":"REGISTERED"}]` heartbeats for every NF. |
| `172.18.0.14` | **AMF** | Owns the N2 SCTP association on port 38412; `amfInstanceId` `e44528d2-9746-41f1-bb50-936d1b93cf87`; callbacks under `/namf-callback/v1/`. Target of TR-N2-001. |
| `172.18.0.15` | **gNB** (UERANSIM `nr-gnb`) — later reused | Holds the N2 association to the AMF and the N3 GTP-U tunnel to the UPF; binds UDP/4997. From module5 the same address is reused by an ephemeral tools container (see TR-VIRT-005). |
| `172.18.0.16` | **UE** (UERANSIM `nr-ue`) | Speaks RLS to the gNB on UDP/4997; source of the storm in TR-RAN-002. |
| `172.18.0.17` | Ephemeral `open5gs-tools` container (module0) | Short-lived; queries `172.18.0.2:27017` for `subscribers`, `security`, `subscriber_status`. |

### Reference points observed

| Interface | Transport | Endpoints | Protection observed |
|---|---|---|---|
| N2 (NGAP) | SCTP/38412, PPID 60 | gNB `.15` ↔ AMF `.14` | **None.** No IPsec, no DTLS. |
| N3 (GTP-U) | UDP/2152 | gNB `.15` ↔ UPF `.12` | **None.** |
| N4 (PFCP) | UDP/8805 | SMF `.9` ↔ UPF `.12` | **None.** No IPsec, no PFCP association security. |
| SBI (HTTP/2) | TCP/7777 | all NFs ↔ SCP `.10` ↔ NRF `.13` | **None.** Cleartext `h2c`; JSON bodies fully readable. |
| UDR backing store | TCP/27017 | UDR `.4`, WebUI `.5`, tools `.17`/`.15` ↔ MongoDB `.2` | **None.** No authentication handshake at all. |
| RLS (radio link sim) | UDP/4997 | UE `.16` ↔ gNB `.15` | Not applicable (simulation channel). |

---

## 3. Management assets (`10.10.0.0/16`)

| Address | Role | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| `10.10.2.10` | Operator workstation | Originates SSH to the capture host; 7,267 packets to `10.10.3.20:22` in module8 alone. |
| `10.10.3.20` | **Container host / capture host** | Runs every `tcpdump -i any -w moduleN.pcap` in the dataset; runs the Docker daemon; source of the `ping 8.8.8.8` used to generate the `ICMP_FLOOD` class. |
| `10.10.3.254` | DNS resolver / gateway | Destination of 1,338–3,989 TCP SYNs to port 53 per capture. |
| `10.10.4.10` | **Zabbix + Wazuh** monitoring | TCP/10051 (Zabbix server) and TCP/1514 (Wazuh agent) from the capture host, continuously across all nine captures. |

The monitoring stack is worth noting: Zabbix and Wazuh are both present and both active
throughout, yet nothing in the dataset indicates that any of the 27 catalogued behaviours raised
an alert. The 5G control plane is simply outside their visibility — they watch hosts, not N2/N4.
That gap is the subject of [DETECTION.md](DETECTION.md).

---

## 4. Subscriber population

Six subscriber records are recoverable from the database response in `module5.pcap` frame 5243,
and three of them are seen registering in the captures.

| IMSI (pseudonym) | Observed UE IPs | Seen registering |
|---|---|---|
| `999702813335208` | `10.45.0.7`, `10.45.0.11`, `10.45.0.12` | yes (module0, module1) |
| `999704314796334` | `10.45.0.6`, `10.45.0.10` | yes (module0, module1) |
| `999705145365381` | `10.45.0.8`, `10.45.0.9`, `10.45.0.13` | yes (module0, module1) |
| `999709275422922` | — | provisioned only |
| `999707253793452` | — | provisioned only |
| `999702047615749` | — | provisioned only |

PLMN is MCC `999` / MNC `70` (a test PLMN). Slice is SST 1, DNN `internet`. UE address pool is
`10.45.0.0/24`.

The IMSI, IMEISV and key values above are pseudonyms; the `172.18.0.0/16` addresses are the
dataset's original RFC 1918 Docker addresses, which the anonymiser deliberately left in place.

**Every one of the six records carries the same `k` and the same `opc`.** That is visible
directly in the database response: `716443940B35EC8A7EDA57F98A9A70E6` and
`A14B42703DEBABBE206D7597D93D65FA` repeat for all six subscribers. In a laboratory this is a
convenience; in production it would mean one compromised SIM compromises the entire subscriber
base. It is recorded as part of TR-SUB-001.

Device identities observed as `pei` values in Nudm_UECM registration:
`imeisv-4370816141361512`, `imeisv-4370816144617407`, `imeisv-4370816162227071`.

| Component | Evidence |
|---|---|
| 5G core | Open5GS — `open5gs.subscribers` collection name, `/etc/open5gs/smf.yaml` paths in shell history, port 7777 SBI convention, `ogstun` interface. |
| RAN simulator | UERANSIM — `nr-ue`/`nr-gnb` binaries, `uesimtun0..3` interfaces, `gnbSearchList` config key, RLS on UDP/4997. |
| Subscriber store | MongoDB, accessed by `mongosh 2.4.2` on `Node.js v20.18.3`. |
| Orchestration | Docker Compose — `ngc.yaml` (core), `g1b1.yaml` (RAN), `register_subscriber.sh`. |
| Offensive tooling | Montimage 5GReplay v0.0.1; `arp-scan`; `tshark`; an unrecovered `pfcpExploit.py`. |
| ML pipeline | Python 3, pandas, scikit-learn (`RandomForestClassifier`), Jupyter; scripts `preprocess.py`, `train_ids.py`, `train_poisoned_ids.py`. |
| Monitoring | Zabbix (10051), Wazuh (1514). |
